  2. Short selling would be forbidden
  3. The model would become nonlinear
  4. The duals would no longer be available
Explanation. The default lower bound is 0: the positions could not be negative, so strategies that sell short (such as borrowing at the risk-free rate) would be excluded and many arbitrages not detected.
lpm-f-214 · level facile · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R = 1 + r$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. The case-study script adds the normalization $\sum_i s^0_ix_i \ge -1$ only when requested (`normalizza`). What is reported for prices $(6, 20)$ without it?
  1. Status `UNBOUNDED`
  2. Status `INFEASIBLE`: no strategy
  3. Optimum $0$: no arbitrage
  4. Optimum $-1$, as when normalized
Explanation. Without normalization the LP with an arbitrage of type A has no finite optimum: the status is `UNBOUNDED`; with it, optimum $-1$ and payoffs $(0;\ 0;\ 0.20) \ge 0$.
lpm-f-215 · level facile · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R = 1 + r$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. In the case-study code the state constraints are kept in `v_stato = m.addConstrs(...)`. Why?
  1. To delete them after the solve
  2. Because `addConstrs` requires it
  3. To read their duals `Pi`
  4. To print them in the solver log
Explanation. The duals of the state constraints are the pricing measure $p_j$; multiplied by $R$ they give the risk-neutral probabilities.
lpm-f-216 · level facile · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R = 1 + r$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. Three-state market of the lab ($R = 1.04$): payoffs of security 1 $(10, 15, 13)$, of security 2 $(30, 15, 25)$. Consistent prices $(1;\ 13;\ 18.6923)$: the duals of the state constraints are $p^* = (0.2846;\ 0.6769;\ 0)$. What is the risk-neutral measure $q$?
  1. $q = p^* = (0.2846;\ 0.6769;\ 0)$
  2. $q = (1/3;\ 1/3;\ 1/3)$
  3. $q = p^*/R = (0.274;\ 0.651;\ 0)$
  4. $q = R\,p^* = (0.296;\ 0.704;\ 0)$
Explanation. The dual constraint of the risk-free security gives $\sum_jp_j = 1/R$, so $q_j = R\,p_j$ are probabilities: $1.04\cdot 0.2846 = 0.296$ and $1.04\cdot 0.6769 = 0.704$.
lpm-f-217 · level facile · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R = 1 + r$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. Three-state market of the lab ($R = 1.04$): payoffs of security 1 $(10, 15, 13)$, of security 2 $(30, 15, 25)$. With $q = (0.296;\ 0.704;\ 0)$, how is the price of security 2 today checked?
  1. $\sum_jq_js^1_{2j} = 18.69$ undiscounted
  2. $\frac{1}{1.04}(0.296\cdot 30 + 0.704\cdot 15) = 18.69$
  3. $\frac13(30 + 15 + 25) = 23.33$
  4. $1.04\,(0.296\cdot 30 + 0.704\cdot 15) = 20.22$
Explanation. The price today is the discounted expected payoff under $q$: $(8.88 + 10.56)/1.04 = 18.69$, the quoted $s^0_2$.
lpm-f-218 · level facile · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R = 1 + r$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. Three-state market of the lab ($R = 1.04$): payoffs of security 1 $(10, 15, 13)$, of security 2 $(30, 15, 25)$. Consistent prices $(1;\ 13;\ 18.6923)$, optimum of the arbitrage LP $= 0$. What does it mean?
  1. The prices are consistent: no arbitrage
  2. Payoffs strictly positive in every state: arbitrage
  3. The LP is infeasible
  4. A negative payoff in state 3
Explanation. With optimum 0 no strategy has negative cost and nonnegative payoffs: the prices are consistent, and the duals of the active state constraints price the securities.
lpm-f-219 · level facile · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R = 1 + r$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. Three-state market of the lab ($R = 1.04$): payoffs of security 1 $(10, 15, 13)$, of security 2 $(30, 15, 25)$. Only securities 0 and 1 are quoted (prices 1 and 13). How do the notes compute the interval of prices of security 2 that create no arbitrage?
  1. By solving the arbitrage LP with random prices
  2. By averaging the payoffs of security 2
  3. Min and max of its value over consistent $p$
  4. With one LP that maximizes the price
Explanation. Over the pricing measures $p \ge 0$ that reproduce the quoted prices, minimizing and maximizing the value of security 2 gives $[18.69;\ 21.54]$.
lpm-f-220 · level ipotesi · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R = 1 + r$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. A call on security 1 with strike 12 pays $(0, 3, 1)$ in the three states. With securities 0, 1, 2 quoted its price is unique, $2.0308$; with only 0 and 1 it is an interval, $[1.4615;\ 2.0308]$. Which hypothesis makes the price unique?
  1. A positive risk-free rate
  2. A complete market
  3. A payoff of the call that is never zero
  4. Real probabilities known in advance
Explanation. With three independent securities in three states the dual system $\sum_js^1_{ij}p_j = s^0_i$ has a unique solution and every new security has one price; with fewer securities (incomplete market) the consistent measures are infinite and the price is an interval.
lpm-f-221 · level media · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R = 1 + r$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. Three-state market of the lab ($R = 1.04$): payoffs of security 1 $(10, 15, 13)$, of security 2 $(30, 15, 25)$. Prices today $s^0_1 = 6$, $s^0_2 = 20$. One buys one unit of each risky security (spending 26) and borrows $B$ at the risk-free rate ($x_0 = -B$). What is the largest $B$ for which the payoffs stay $\ge 0$ in every state?
  1. $B = 30$
  2. $B = 38.46$
  3. $B = 27$
  4. $B = 28.85$
Explanation. Payoff in state $j$: $s^1_{1j} + s^1_{2j} - 1.04B \ge 0$, i.e. $B \le \min(40, 30, 38)/1.04 = 30/1.04 = 28.85$; the worst state binds. Today one cashes in up to $28.85 - 26 = 2.85$ with no risk.

Part: Optimization and machine learning (45)

11. Robust and quantile regression (45)

lpm-f-076 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. With $\tau = 1/2$, what does the objective become?
  1. Half the sum of absolute deviations: median regression
  2. The sum of the squared deviations
  3. The largest absolute deviation
  4. The sum of the deviations with their sign
Explanation. Deviations above and below cost the same, $1/2$ each, and at the optimum $u_i + v_i = |y_i - \text{prediction}|$.
lpm-f-077 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. Let $\tilde\pi_i$ be the optimal dual of the equality constraint of observation $i$. In which interval does it lie?
  1. $[0, 1]$
  2. $[-1, 1]$
  3. $[-(1 - \tau),\ \tau]$
  4. $[-\tau,\ 1 - \tau]$
Explanation. From the dual constraints of $u_i$ and $v_i$: $\tilde\pi_i = \tau$ above the estimate, $-(1 - \tau)$ below, an interior value on the interpolated points.
lpm-f-078 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. With $p$ features, what are the support points of the optimal estimate?
  1. The observations with the largest deviations
  2. The outliers, such as the strike days
  3. All the observations below the estimate
  4. The interpolated observations (usually $p + 1$), with interior duals
Explanation. Moving any other observation, as long as it stays on its side of the estimate, changes nothing.
lpm-f-079 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. Feature selection adds a budget $\sum_j z_j \le t$ with $z_j \ge |w_j|$. Why must the features be standardized first?
  1. To make the LP feasible for every budget $t$
  2. Otherwise the budget adds coefficients in different units
  3. To make all the coefficients $w_j$ positive
  4. Because Gurobi requires standardized data
Explanation. Without standardization the selection would depend on the units of measurement; after it each coefficient reads as pieces per standard deviation of the feature.
lpm-f-080 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. With the budget $\sum_j |w_j| \le t$ on standardized coefficients, the shadow price of the budget goes from $-19.95$ to $-4.20$ pieces per unit as $t$ grows. How is it read?
  1. As the price of complexity: first units buy signal, the last ones noise
  2. As the coefficient of temperature in the fitted model
  3. As the number of selected features
  4. As the error made on the strike days
Explanation. The first units of budget buy temperature, which explains almost everything; the budget to choose is the one beyond which the shadow price becomes negligible.
lpm-f-081 · level facile · notes
A bakery orders the critical-fractile quantile of demand (newsvendor rule). For a hot weekend day the quantile of the whole history says $148$ pieces, the quantile regression conditioned on the day says $132$. What is the gain?
  1. None: the two numbers are equivalent
  2. A lower selling price for the bakery
  3. About 16 fewer unsold pieces, with the same costs and rule
  4. A higher critical fractile
Explanation. The quantile rule still holds, applied to the demand conditional on the day, using information already in hand; the gap between median and critical fractile is the safety stock.
lpm-f-082 · level teorema · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. The dual constraint of the free intercept $b$ is $\sum_i\tilde\pi_i = 0$. What does it express?
  1. That the deviations sum to zero
  2. The quantile property: about a fraction $\tau$ of the data below the estimate
  3. That the estimate passes through the mean of the data
  4. That all the duals are zero
Explanation. With $\tilde\pi_i = \tau$ above, $-(1 - \tau)$ below and interior values on the $p + 1$ support points: $\tau n_+ - (1 - \tau)n_- + \sum_{\text{interp.}}\tilde\pi_i = 0$; with $\tau = 1/2$ half above and half below.
lpm-f-083 · level ipotesi · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. Why is at least one of $\tilde u_i$, $\tilde v_i$ zero at the optimum?
  1. Because $u_i$ and $v_i$ are binary variables
  2. Because the intercept $b$ is free
  3. Because the features are standardized
  4. $\tau, 1 - \tau > 0$: lowering both by the same amount keeps the constraint, lowers the cost
Explanation. Same positive-part reasoning as in the newsvendor: the split of the deviation into $u_i - v_i$ is tight at the optimum.
lpm-f-120 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression for a bakery: for a hot weekend day the median model ($\tau = 0.5$) predicts $126.3$ pieces and the model at the critical fractile $\tau = 9/13$ predicts $132.1$. What is the safety stock, and how is it obtained?
  1. $5.8$ pieces: the quantile prediction minus the median one
  2. $132.1$ pieces: the whole quantile prediction
  3. $16$ pieces: the gap with the unconditional quantile
  4. $0$: no distribution was assumed
Explanation. The safety stock is computed directly as the gap between the two conditional estimates, with no assumption on the distribution of demand.
lpm-f-121 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. With no features ($p = 0$) only the intercept $b$ is left. What does the LP return as $\tilde b$?
  1. The mean of the observations
  2. An empirical $\tau$-quantile of the observations
  3. The median, whatever $\tau$
  4. The largest observation
Explanation. Raising $b$ pays while the share of observations below it is at most $\tau$: the model *is* the quantile rule; with features it gives a conditional quantile.
lpm-f-159 · level facile · notes
The lab chapter on robust and quantile regression starts from the parameters used by the other models (demand curves, costs, scenarios). What is its point?
  1. Those parameters are given and need no discussion
  2. Estimating them is itself an optimization problem
  3. They are always estimated by least squares
  4. They can be estimated only with a machine-learning library
Explanation. Someone must estimate the numbers that feed the models, and the estimate is an optimization problem: with absolute deviations it stays an LP, solved with Gurobi as everything else.
lpm-f-160 · level facile · notes
In the lab, a demand forecast is estimated by minimizing the sum of absolute deviations instead of squared ones. Which two advantages do the notes give?
  1. A closed-form solution and a unique optimum
  2. Smaller errors on every observation and faster solves
  3. No need for data and no need for a solver
  4. It stays an LP and anomalous days weigh less
Explanation. Absolute deviations keep the problem linear (two nonnegative parts per deviation) and pay each deviation once, so a few anomalous days cannot pull the line; weighting the two parts asymmetrically aims at a quantile.
lpm-f-161 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ ($i = 1, \dots, n$) with features $x_{ij}$ ($j = 1, \dots, p$), free coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. How many variables and equality constraints does the LP have?
  1. $p$ variables and $n + 1$ constraints
  2. $n + p$ variables and $2n$ constraints
  3. $2n + p + 1$ variables and $n$ constraints
  4. $2n$ variables and $p + 1$ constraints
Explanation. Free variables: the $p$ coefficients and the intercept; nonnegative: $u_i$ and $v_i$ for each observation; one equality constraint per observation defines its deviation.
lpm-f-162 · level media · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ ($i = 1, \dots, n$) with features $x_{ij}$ ($j = 1, \dots, p$), free coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. No features ($p = 0$): seven days of sales $88, 96, 104, 112, 120, 132, 148$ and $\tau = 9/13$ (bakery with $c_u = 9$, $c_o = 4$). What are the optimal intercept $\tilde b$ and the optimal value?
  1. $\tilde b = 120$, value $680/13 \approx 52.31$
  2. $\tilde b = 112$, value $768/13 \approx 59.08$
  3. $\tilde b = 132$, value $704/13 \approx 54.15$
  4. $\tilde b = 114.29$ (the mean), value $\approx 57.14$
Explanation. Raising $b$ saves $\tau$ on each observation above and costs $1 - \tau$ on each one below: it pays while the share below is at most $\tau$. $\tilde b = 120$ (4 below, 2 above, $4/7 \le \tau \le 5/7$); value $\tfrac4{13}(32 + 24 + 16 + 8) + \tfrac9{13}(12 + 28) = 680/13$.

lpm-f-164 · level media · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ ($i = 1, \dots, n$) with features $x_{ij}$ ($j = 1, \dots, p$), free coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$.   1. $9/13$
  2. $-4/13$
  3. $-2/13$
  4. $2/13$

lpm-f-165 · level teorema · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ ($i = 1, \dots, n$) with features $x_{ij}$ ($j = 1, \dots, p$), free coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. Let $\tilde\pi_i$ be the optimal dual of the constraint of observation $i$. Optimality on the free coefficient $w_j$ gives $\sum_i\tilde\pi_ix_{ij} = 0$. What does it express?
  1. Feature $j$ must be standardized
  2. Deviation signs are unrelated to feature $j$
  3. Coefficient $w_j$ is zero at the optimum
  4. Exactly half of the observations are above the estimate
Explanation. A free variable gives an equality dual constraint. Since $\tilde\pi_i$ is $\tau$ or $-(1 - \tau)$ according to the side of the deviation, the condition says that the deviations' signs, weighted by $x_{ij}$, balance out: the analogue, feature by feature, of the quantile property given by the intercept.

lpm-f-168 · level facile · notes

Explanation. Each $\tau$ estimates a different conditional quantile, and a feature may shift the low part of the distribution more than the high part: quantile regression describes the whole conditional distribution, not just its center.
lpm-f-169 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ ($i = 1, \dots, n$) with features $x_{ij}$ ($j = 1, \dots, p$), free coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. Feature selection adds variables $z_j \ge 0$, constraints $-z_j \le w_j \le z_j$ and the budget $\sum_jz_j \le t$. Why does $\tilde z_j = |\tilde w_j|$ hold at the optimum?
  1. Because $z_j$ appears in the objective with a positive cost
  2. Because the constraints force $z_j = w_j$
  3. Because the features are standardized
  4. Because extra $z_j$ only wastes budget
Explanation. The two constraints say $z_j \ge |w_j|$; any $z_j$ above $|w_j|$ wastes budget that could relax another coefficient, so at the optimum it can be taken equal to $|w_j|$ and the model stays an LP.
lpm-f-170 · level facile · notes

Bakery, budget $\sum_j|w_j| \le t$ on standardized coefficients, three true features and four irrelevant ones. As $t$ grows, temperature enters at $t = 3$, price at $t = 24$, weekend at $t = 30$. What does the order of entry tell?

  2. The order in which the data were collected
  3. Which features are measured in the largest units
  4. Nothing: it is arbitrary for an LP
Explanation. With a scarce budget the LP spends it where it reduces the error most: the true features enter in order of importance, while the irrelevant ones stay near zero until the budget becomes clearly excessive.

With a budget $t$ on the standardized coefficients, how do the notes choose $t$?

  3. The $t$ beyond which its shadow price is negligible
  4. Always $t$ equal to the number of features
Explanation. The historical error always decreases with $t$ (more freedom chases noise); the shadow price, the price of complexity, says when an extra unit of budget stops buying signal.
lpm-f-172 · level teorema · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ ($i = 1, \dots, n$) with features $x_{ij}$ ($j = 1, \dots, p$), free coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. With the budget constraint $\sum_jz_j \le t$ ($z_j \ge |w_j|$), the optimal mean absolute deviation as a function of $t$ is decreasing and convex. Which result explains the convexity?
  1. The features are standardized
  2. Convexity of an LP's value in a right-hand side
  3. The quantile property of the intercept
  4. Strong duality makes every value function linear
Explanation. For a minimization LP, $\tilde z(t)$ is the maximum of the dual objective over a fixed dual feasible set, linear in $t$: a maximum of linear functions is convex. The slope is the shadow price, which rises from $-19.95$ to $-4.20$.

  4. So that fewer days of history are needed
Explanation. Absolute deviations are linearized with two non-negative variables: the model is an LP, and every deviation weighs in proportion to its size, so a few anomalous days do not drag the estimate.

2. Background: linear programming and sensitivity analysis (6)

Example 1 · lpm-g-lp-duali-cs · level facile · notes
Consider the LP $\max\; 43x_1 + 73x_2$ subject to $x_1 + 4x_2 \le 47$, $3x_1 + 3x_2 \le 96$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$; its dual has one variable $\pi_i \ge 0$ per constraint $i$ (the shadow price of the constraint). The optimal solution is $(\tilde x_1, \tilde x_2) = (27, 5)$, with both constraints active. What are the optimal dual values $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2)$?
  1. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (18.11; 6.22)$
  2. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (11; 10)$
  3. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (10; 11)$
  4. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (10.75; 10.43)$
Explanation. Both variables are positive, so by complementary slackness both dual constraints are active: $1\pi_1 + 3\pi_2 = 43$ and $4\pi_1 + 3\pi_2 = 73$ (the coefficients are the columns of the primal), hence $\tilde\pi = (10; 11)$. Solving with the rows instead of the columns is the typical mistake.
Example 2 · lpm-g-lp-duali-cs · level facile · notes
Consider the LP $\max\; 32x_1 + 49x_2$ subject to $2x_1 + x_2 \le 72$, $2x_1 + 4x_2 \le 138$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$; its dual has one variable $\pi_i \ge 0$ per constraint $i$ (the shadow price of the constraint). The optimal solution is $(\tilde x_1, \tilde x_2) = (25, 22)$, with both constraints active. What are the optimal dual values $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2)$?
  1. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (5; 11)$
  2. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (13.17; 5.67)$
  3. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (11; 5)$
  4. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (8; 9.8)$
Explanation. Both variables are positive, so by complementary slackness both dual constraints are active: $2\pi_1 + 2\pi_2 = 32$ and $1\pi_1 + 4\pi_2 = 49$ (the coefficients are the columns of the primal), hence $\tilde\pi = (5; 11)$. Solving with the rows instead of the columns is the typical mistake.
Example 1 · lpm-g-lp-costo-ridotto · level facile · notes
Consider the LP $\max\; 43x_1 + 73x_2$ subject to $x_1 + 4x_2 \le 47$, $3x_1 + 3x_2 \le 96$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$; its dual has one variable $\pi_i \ge 0$ per constraint $i$ (the shadow price of the constraint). At the optimum the duals are $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2) = (10, 11)$. We add a variable $x_3 \ge 0$ with objective coefficient $c_3 = 49$ and consumptions $a_{13} = 4$, $a_{23} = 1$. What are its reduced cost $\bar c_3$ ($c_3$ minus the value of the resources it consumes at the shadow prices) and the threshold $c_3^{\max}$, the smallest $c_3$ at which $x_3$ becomes worth using?
  1. $\bar c_3 = 2$, threshold $c_3^{\max} = 51$
  2. $\bar c_3 = -5$, threshold $c_3^{\max} = 54$
  3. $\bar c_3 = 9$, threshold $c_3^{\max} = 40$
  4. $\bar c_3 = -2$, threshold $c_3^{\max} = 51$
Explanation. The resources absorbed by one unit of $x_3$ are worth $4\cdot 10 + 1\cdot 11 = 51$ at the shadow prices, so $\bar c_3 = 49 - 51 = -2$: $x_3$ stays at zero and becomes worth activating only if $c_3$ rises to at least $51$.

Example 1 · lpm-g-lp-intervallo-b · level media · notes
Consider the LP $\max\; 43x_1 + 73x_2$ subject to $x_1 + 4x_2 \le 47$, $3x_1 + 3x_2 \le 96$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$; its dual has one variable $\pi_i \ge 0$ per constraint $i$ (the shadow price of the constraint). At the optimum both constraints are active and $\tilde x = (27, 5)$, with $\tilde x_1, \tilde x_2 > 0$. Keeping $b_2 = 96$ fixed, for which values of $b_1$ does this basis stay optimal (the validity range of the shadow price $\tilde\pi_1$)?
  1. $b_1 \in [24; 96]$
  2. $b_1 \in [35.25; 141]$
  3. $b_1 \in [32; 128]$
  4. $b_1 \in [32; 47]$
Explanation. With both constraints active, $x(b_1)$ solves $A x = (b_1, 96)$: $x_1 = (3b_1 - 384)/(-9)$ and $x_2 = (96 - 3b_1)/(-9)$. The basis stays optimal (the duals do not change) as long as both are $\ge 0$, that is for $b_1 \in [32, 128]$; Gurobi returns it as `SARHSLow`–`SARHSUp`.
Example 2 · lpm-g-lp-intervallo-b · level media · notes
Consider the LP $\max\; 32x_1 + 49x_2$ subject to $2x_1 + x_2 \le 72$, $2x_1 + 4x_2 \le 138$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$; its dual has one variable $\pi_i \ge 0$ per constraint $i$ (the shadow price of the constraint). At the optimum both constraints are active and $\tilde x = (25, 22)$, with $\tilde x_1, \tilde x_2 > 0$. Keeping $b_2 = 138$ fixed, for which values of $b_1$ does this basis stay optimal (the validity range of the shadow price $\tilde\pi_1$)?
  1. $b_1 \in [34.5; 138]$
  2. $b_1 \in [69; 276]$
  3. $b_1 \in [72; 288]$
  4. $b_1 \in [34.5; 72]$
Explanation. With both constraints active, $x(b_1)$ solves $A x = (b_1, 138)$: $x_1 = (4b_1 - 138)/6$ and $x_2 = (276 - 2b_1)/6$. The basis stays optimal (the duals do not change) as long as both are $\ge 0$, that is for $b_1 \in [34.5, 138]$; Gurobi returns it as `SARHSLow`–`SARHSUp`.
Example 1 · lpm-g-lp-dualita-debole · level facile · notes
Consider the LP $\max\; 43x_1 + 73x_2$ subject to $x_1 + 4x_2 \le 47$, $3x_1 + 3x_2 \le 96$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$; its dual has one variable $\pi_i \ge 0$ per constraint $i$ (the shadow price of the constraint). The point $\bar x = (23, 4)$ is feasible for the primal and $\bar\pi = (11, 11)$ is feasible for the dual $\min\ 47\pi_1 + 96\pi_2$ (dual constraints $\pi_1 + 3\pi_2 \ge 43$, $4\pi_1 + 3\pi_2 \ge 73$). By weak duality, which interval certainly contains the optimal value $\tilde z$ of the primal?
  1. $\tilde z \in [989; 1281]$
  2. $\tilde z \in [1573; 1865]$
  3. $\tilde z \in [1281; 1427]$
  4. $\tilde z \in [1281; 1573]$
Explanation. Every feasible primal point gives a lower bound for a maximum, $43\cdot 23 + 73\cdot 4 = 1281$, and every feasible dual point an upper bound, $47\cdot 11 + 96\cdot 11 = 1573$. So $1281 \le \tilde z \le 1573$ (here $\tilde z = 1526$). Swapping the roles of the two bounds is the typical mistake.
Example 2 · lpm-g-lp-dualita-debole · level facile · notes

By weak duality, which interval certainly contains the optimal value $\tilde z$ of the primal?
  1. $\tilde z \in [672; 1554]$
  2. $\tilde z \in [2436; 3318]$
  3. $\tilde z \in [1554; 2436]$
  4. $\tilde z \in [1554; 1995]$
Explanation. Every feasible primal point gives a lower bound for a maximum, $32\cdot 21 + 49\cdot 18 = 1554$, and every feasible dual point an upper bound, $72\cdot 7 + 138\cdot 14 = 2436$. So $1554 \le \tilde z \le 2436$ (here $\tilde z = 1878$). Swapping the roles of the two bounds is the typical mistake.
Example 1 · lpm-g-lp-perturbazione · level facile · notes
Consider the LP $\max\; 43x_1 + 73x_2$ subject to $x_1 + 4x_2 \le 47$, $3x_1 + 3x_2 \le 96$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$; its dual has one variable $\pi_i \ge 0$ per constraint $i$ (the shadow price of the constraint). At the optimum $\tilde z = 1526$, the shadow price of constraint 1 is $\tilde\pi_1 = 10$ (and $\tilde\pi_2 = 11$), and the validity range of $b_1$ (where $\tilde\pi_1$ stays exact) is $[32; 128]$. What is the new optimal value if $b_1$ changes from $47$ to $57$, all else equal?
  1. $\tilde z = 1426$
  2. $\tilde z = 1536$
  3. $\tilde z = 1636$
  4. $\tilde z = 1626$
Explanation. The new value $57$ is inside the validity range, so the shadow price is exact: $\tilde z + \tilde\pi_1\,\Delta b_1 = 1526 + 10\cdot(10) = 1626$.

Example 1 · lpm-g-lp-tutti-casi · level complessa · notes
Consider the LP $\min\ -4x_1 + 12x_2 - 12x_3$, subject to (1) $x_1 + x_2 \ge 39$, (2) $x_1 + x_2 - x_3 = 64$, (3) $3x_1 - x_2 \le 92$, $x_1 \ge 0$, $x_2$ free, $x_3 \le 0$. Its optimal solution is $\tilde x = (39, 25, 0)$. Let $\tilde\pi_i = \partial\tilde z/\partial b_i$ be the dual value of constraint $i$ (in a minimum: $\pi_i \ge 0$ for $\ge$, free for $=$, $\le 0$ for $\le$). Using complementary slackness, what are the optimal dual values?
  1. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; 12; -5.33)$
  2. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; 2; -6)$
  3. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; 8; -4)$
  4. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; -4; 8)$
Explanation. Constraint (1) is slack ($64 > 39$), so $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$. $x_1 > 0$ and $x_2 \ne 0$ make their dual constraints active (columns of the primal): $\pi_2 + 3\pi_3 = -4$ and $\pi_2 - \pi_3 = 12$, so $\tilde\pi_2 = 8$, $\tilde\pi_3 = -4 \le 0$ as required. The dual constraint of $x_3$ need not be active: $x_3$ sits at its bound $0$ with reduced cost $-12 + 8 = -4$. Check: $\sum_i b_i\tilde\pi_i = 144 = \tilde z$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-lp-tutti-casi · level complessa · notes
Consider the LP $\min\ 4x_1 + 16x_2 - 12x_3$, subject to (1) $x_1 + x_2 \ge 51$, (2) $x_1 + x_2 - x_3 = 64$, (3) $x_1 - 2x_2 \le -41$, $x_1 \ge 0$, $x_2$ free, $x_3 \le 0$. Its optimal solution is $\tilde x = (29, 35, 0)$. Let $\tilde\pi_i = \partial\tilde z/\partial b_i$ be the dual value of constraint $i$ (in a minimum: $\pi_i \ge 0$ for $\ge$, free for $=$, $\le 0$ for $\le$). Using complementary slackness, what are the optimal dual values?
  1. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; 8; -4)$
  2. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; 12; -8)$
  3. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; -4; 8)$
  4. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; 8; 4)$
Explanation. Constraint (1) is slack ($64 > 51$), so $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$. $x_1 > 0$ and $x_2 \ne 0$ make their dual constraints active (columns of the primal): $\pi_2 + \pi_3 = 4$ and $\pi_2 - 2\pi_3 = 16$, so $\tilde\pi_2 = 8$, $\tilde\pi_3 = -4 \le 0$ as required. The dual constraint of $x_3$ need not be active: $x_3$ sits at its bound $0$ with reduced cost $-12 + 8 = -4$. Check: $\sum_i b_i\tilde\pi_i = 676 = \tilde z$.

3. The solver: building, solving, interpreting (4)

Example 1 · lpm-g-sv-dimensioni · level facile · notes
A gurobipy production-inventory LP has $n = 4$ products and $m = 8$ months: variables $x_{it} \ge 0$ (units produced) and $s_{it} \ge 0$ (inventory at the end of the month) for every product $i$ and month $t$; one balance constraint $s_{i,t-1} + x_{it} = d_{it} + s_{it}$ per product and month (initial inventory $s_{i0}$ is a datum); one machine-hours constraint $\sum_i a_ix_{it} \le b_t$ per month. What does the line `Optimize a model with ... rows, ... columns` of the Gurobi log report?
  1. $64$ rows, $40$ columns
  2. $40$ rows, $64$ columns
  3. $32$ rows, $64$ columns
  4. $40$ rows, $32$ columns
Explanation. Rows are constraints: $nm = 32$ balances plus $m = 8$ capacities, $40$ in all; columns are variables: $2nm = 64$. Checking these sizes is the first reading of the log.
Example 2 · lpm-g-sv-dimensioni · level facile · notes
A gurobipy production-inventory LP has $n = 3$ products and $m = 11$ months: variables $x_{it} \ge 0$ (units produced) and $s_{it} \ge 0$ (inventory at the end of the month) for every product $i$ and month $t$; one balance constraint $s_{i,t-1} + x_{it} = d_{it} + s_{it}$ per product and month (initial inventory $s_{i0}$ is a datum); one machine-hours constraint $\sum_i a_ix_{it} \le b_t$ per month. What does the line `Optimize a model with ... rows, ... columns` of the Gurobi log report?
  1. $44$ rows, $33$ columns
  2. $33$ rows, $66$ columns
  3. $44$ rows, $66$ columns
  4. $66$ rows, $44$ columns
Explanation. Rows are constraints: $nm = 33$ balances plus $m = 11$ capacities, $44$ in all; columns are variables: $2nm = 66$. Checking these sizes is the first reading of the log.
Example 1 · lpm-g-sv-rc-soglia · level facile · notes
gurobipy, minimization LP: $\min\ 16x_1 + 17x_2$ subject to $2x_1 + 2x_2 \ge 110$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$. At the optimum $x_2 = 0$. What are its reduced cost (attribute `x2.RC`, $c_2$ minus the value of the resources it uses at the shadow prices) and the cost threshold `SAObjLow` below which $x_2$ becomes worth using?
  1. `RC` $= 1$, threshold $16$
  2. `RC` $= 1$, threshold $18$
  3. `RC` $= -1$, threshold $16$
  4. `RC` $= 9$, threshold $8$
Explanation. Only $x_1$ is used, so the shadow price of the constraint is $c_1/a_1 = 8$; $x_2$ uses $2$ units, worth $16$: `RC` $= 17 - 16 = 1 \ge 0$ (a variable at its lower bound in a minimum). It becomes worth using when its cost falls to $16$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-sv-rc-soglia · level facile · notes
gurobipy, minimization LP: $\min\ 8x_1 + 16x_2$ subject to $2x_1 + 3x_2 \ge 30$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$. At the optimum $x_2 = 0$. What are its reduced cost (attribute `x2.RC`, $c_2$ minus the value of the resources it uses at the shadow prices) and the cost threshold `SAObjLow` below which $x_2$ becomes worth using?
  1. `RC` $= 4$, threshold $12$
  2. `RC` $= -4$, threshold $12$
  3. `RC` $= 4$, threshold $20$
  4. `RC` $= 12$, threshold $4$
Explanation. Only $x_1$ is used, so the shadow price of the constraint is $c_1/a_1 = 4$; $x_2$ uses $3$ units, worth $12$: `RC` $= 16 - 12 = 4 \ge 0$ (a variable at its lower bound in a minimum). It becomes worth using when its cost falls to $12$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-sv-pi-capacita · level facile · notes
gurobipy, minimization LP: $\min\ 5x_1 + 11x_2$ subject to demand $x_1 + x_2 \ge 130$ and capacity of the cheap source $x_1 \le 70$, $x \ge 0$. Output: optimal cost $1010$; demand constraint `Pi` $= 11$; capacity constraint `Pi` $= -6$, `SARHS` $= [0, 130]$. What is the optimal cost if the capacity becomes $71$?
  1. $\tilde z = 1004$
  2. $\tilde z = 1015$
  3. $\tilde z = 1016$
  4. $\tilde z = 1021$
Explanation. `Pi` $= \partial\tilde z/\partial b$: a $\le$ capacity in a minimum has `Pi` $\le 0$, each unit saves $6$ (the cheap source replaces the expensive one). The new value is inside `SARHS`, so $1010 + (-6)\cdot(1) = 1004$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-sv-pi-capacita · level facile · notes
gurobipy, minimization LP: $\min\ 4x_1 + 12x_2$ subject to demand $x_1 + x_2 \ge 50$ and capacity of the cheap source $x_1 \le 40$, $x \ge 0$. Output: optimal cost $280$; demand constraint `Pi` $= 12$; capacity constraint `Pi` $= -8$, `SARHS` $= [0, 50]$. What is the optimal cost if the capacity becomes $44$?
  1. $\tilde z = 248$
  2. $\tilde z = 328$
  3. $\tilde z = 312$
  4. $\tilde z = 296$
Explanation. `Pi` $= \partial\tilde z/\partial b$: a $\le$ capacity in a minimum has `Pi` $\le 0$, each unit saves $8$ (the cheap source replaces the expensive one). The new value is inside `SARHS`, so $280 + (-8)\cdot(4) = 248$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-sv-rhs-costante · level facile · notes
In gurobipy the meter constraint of an EV-charging model is written `constr = m.addConstr(quicksum(x[v,t] for v in V) + base[t] <= C)`, with the constant `base[t] = 39` (base load, kW) and `C = 109` (meter limit). What value does Gurobi store in `constr.RHS`, and which value must be assigned to `constr.RHS` to study a meter limit of $134$ kW?
  1. `constr.RHS` $= 148$, to set $173$
  2. `constr.RHS` $= 109$, to set $134$
  3. `constr.RHS` $= 70$, to set $134$
  4. `constr.RHS` $= 70$, to set $95$
Explanation. Gurobi moves the constant to the right-hand side: `RHS` $= 109 - 39 = 70$. Writing `constr.RHS = 134` would impose $\sum_v x_{vt} \le 134$, another constraint; the correct assignment is $134 - 39 = 95$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-sv-rhs-costante · level facile · notes
In gurobipy the meter constraint of an EV-charging model is written `constr = m.addConstr(quicksum(x[v,t] for v in V) + base[t] <= C)`, with the constant `base[t] = 29` (base load, kW) and `C = 119` (meter limit). What value does Gurobi store in `constr.RHS`, and which value must be assigned to `constr.RHS` to study a meter limit of $109$ kW?
  1. `constr.RHS` $= 90$, to set $80$
  2. `constr.RHS` $= 148$, to set $138$
  3. `constr.RHS` $= 90$, to set $109$
  4. `constr.RHS` $= 119$, to set $109$
Explanation. Gurobi moves the constant to the right-hand side: `RHS` $= 119 - 29 = 90$. Writing `constr.RHS = 109` would impose $\sum_v x_{vt} \le 109$, another constraint; the correct assignment is $109 - 29 = 80$.

4. Modelling techniques (7)

Example 1 · lpm-g-modelli-dimensioni · level facile · notes
A company has to satisfy the demand $d_t$ of a product over $n = 8$ periods, with maximum production $p_t$ in every period. In the multi-period LP model, with variables $x_t$ (production of period $t$) and $y_t$ (inventory at the end of period $t$, with $y_0 = 0$ given), one inventory balance constraint and one capacity constraint per period, how many variables and how many constraints (excluding non-negativity) are there?
  1. $8$ variables and $16$ constraints
  2. $16$ variables and $16$ constraints
  3. $16$ variables and $8$ constraints
  4. $17$ variables and $16$ constraints
Explanation. The variables are $x_t$ and $y_t$ for $t = 1, \dots, n$: $2n = 16$ ($y_0 = 0$ is a datum). The constraints are $n$ inventory balance constraints and $n$ capacity constraints: $2n = 16$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-modelli-dimensioni · level facile · notes
A company has to satisfy the demand $d_t$ of a product over $n = 11$ periods, with maximum production $p_t$ in every period. In the multi-period LP model, with variables $x_t$ (production of period $t$) and $y_t$ (inventory at the end of period $t$, with $y_0 = 0$ given), one inventory balance constraint and one capacity constraint per period, how many variables and how many constraints (excluding non-negativity) are there?
  1. $23$ variables and $22$ constraints
  2. $22$ variables and $11$ constraints
  3. $22$ variables and $22$ constraints
  4. $11$ variables and $22$ constraints
Explanation. The variables are $x_t$ and $y_t$ for $t = 1, \dots, n$: $2n = 22$ ($y_0 = 0$ is a datum). The constraints are $n$ inventory balance constraints and $n$ capacity constraints: $2n = 22$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-modello-produzione · level media · notes
A company makes two products using two resources, available in quantities $13$ (resource 1) and $20$ (resource 2). Profits and consumptions per unit of product are: $\begin{array}{l|cc} & \text{product 1} & \text{product 2} \\ \hline \text{unit profit} & 9 & 2 \\ \text{resource 1 per unit} & 5 & 1 \\ \text{resource 2 per unit} & 2 & 1 \end{array}$. With $x_j \ge 0$ units of product $j$, which LP model maximizes the total profit?
  1. $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 9\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 5\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \le 13 \\ & x_1 + x_2 \le 20 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  2. $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 2\,x_1 + 9\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 5\,x_1 + x_2 \le 13 \\ & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \le 20 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  3. $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 9\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 5\,x_1 + x_2 \le 13 \\ & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \le 20 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  4. $\begin{array}{rl} \min & 9\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 5\,x_1 + x_2 \ge 13 \\ & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \ge 20 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
Explanation. Objective function: total profit $9\,x_1 + 2\,x_2$ to maximize. One constraint per resource: the consumption $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j$ (row $i$ of the table, not column) does not exceed the availability $b_i$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-modello-produzione · level media · notes
A company makes two products using two resources, available in quantities $34$ (resource 1) and $30$ (resource 2). Profits and consumptions per unit of product are: $\begin{array}{l|cc} & \text{product 1} & \text{product 2} \\ \hline \text{unit profit} & 5 & 8 \\ \text{resource 1 per unit} & 2 & 1 \\ \text{resource 2 per unit} & 2 & 5 \end{array}$. With $x_j \ge 0$ units of product $j$, which LP model maximizes the total profit?
  1. $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 5\,x_1 + 8\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \le 34 \\ & 2\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 \le 30 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  2. $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 5\,x_1 + 8\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,x_1 + 2\,x_2 \le 34 \\ & x_1 + 5\,x_2 \le 30 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  3. $\begin{array}{rl} \min & 5\,x_1 + 8\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \ge 34 \\ & 2\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 \ge 30 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
  4. $\begin{array}{rl} \max & 8\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 \\ \text{subject to} & 2\,x_1 + x_2 \le 34 \\ & 2\,x_1 + 5\,x_2 \le 30 \\ & x_1, x_2 \ge 0 \end{array}$
Explanation. Objective function: total profit $5\,x_1 + 8\,x_2$ to maximize. One constraint per resource: the consumption $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j$ (row $i$ of the table, not column) does not exceed the availability $b_i$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-mod-minmax · level facile · notes
In an LP model with ${\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and other linear constraints, the objective is $\min\ \max\{3\,x_1 + x_2,~ 3\,x_1 + 5\,x_2\}$. With a new free variable $w$, which linear formulation is equivalent?
  1. $\min\ w$, $w \ge 3\,x_1 + x_2$, $w \ge 3\,x_1 + 5\,x_2$
  2. $\max\ w$, $w \ge 3\,x_1 + x_2$, $w \ge 3\,x_1 + 5\,x_2$
  3. $\min\ w$, $w \le 3\,x_1 + x_2$, $w \le 3\,x_1 + 5\,x_2$
  4. $\max\ w$, $w \le 3\,x_1 + x_2$, $w \le 3\,x_1 + 5\,x_2$
Explanation. $w$ must not be less than either function; minimizing it gives their maximum.
Example 2 · lpm-g-mod-minmax · level facile · notes
In an LP model with ${\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ and other linear constraints, the objective is $\min\ \max\{2\,x_1 + 3\,x_2,~ -x_1 + x_2\}$. With a new free variable $w$, which linear formulation is equivalent?
  1. $\min\ w$, $w \ge 2\,x_1 + 3\,x_2$, $w \ge -x_1 + x_2$
  2. $\min\ w$, $w \le 2\,x_1 + 3\,x_2$, $w \le -x_1 + x_2$
  3. $\max\ w$, $w \ge 2\,x_1 + 3\,x_2$, $w \ge -x_1 + x_2$
  4. $\max\ w$, $w \le 2\,x_1 + 3\,x_2$, $w \le -x_1 + x_2$
Explanation. $w$ must not be less than either function; minimizing it gives their maximum.
Example 1 · lpm-g-mod-rapporto · level facile · notes
In a blending model with quantities $x_1, x_2, x_3 \ge 0$ and $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 > 0$, material $2$ must be at most $30\%$ of the total: $\frac{x_{2}}{x_1 + x_2 + x_3} \le \frac{3}{10}$. Which linear constraint is equivalent?
  1. $\frac{3}{10}\,x_1 + \frac{13}{10}\,x_2 + \frac{3}{10}\,x_3 \le 0$
  2. $-\frac{3}{10}\,x_1 + \frac{7}{10}\,x_2 - \frac{3}{10}\,x_3 \le 0$
  3. $\frac{7}{10}\,x_2 \le 0$
  4. $-\frac{3}{10}\,x_1 + \frac{7}{10}\,x_2 - \frac{3}{10}\,x_3 \ge 0$
Explanation. Multiply by the positive denominator: $x_{2} - \frac{3}{10}(x_1 + x_2 + x_3) \le 0$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-mod-rapporto · level facile · notes
In a blending model with quantities $x_1, x_2, x_3 \ge 0$ and $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 > 0$, material $2$ must be at most $40\%$ of the total: $\frac{x_{2}}{x_1 + x_2 + x_3} \le \frac{2}{5}$. Which linear constraint is equivalent?
  1. $-\frac{2}{5}\,x_1 + \frac{3}{5}\,x_2 - \frac{2}{5}\,x_3 \ge 0$
  2. $\frac{3}{5}\,x_2 \le 0$
  3. $-\frac{2}{5}\,x_1 + \frac{3}{5}\,x_2 - \frac{2}{5}\,x_3 \le 0$
  4. $\frac{2}{5}\,x_1 + \frac{7}{5}\,x_2 + \frac{2}{5}\,x_3 \le 0$
Explanation. Multiply by the positive denominator: $x_{2} - \frac{2}{5}(x_1 + x_2 + x_3) \le 0$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-mod-scorte · level facile · notes
In a multi-period model with $3$ periods, $x_t$ is the production and $y_t$ the inventory at the end of period $t$, with initial inventory $y_0 = 1$ and balance $y_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + y_t$. With demands $d = (20, 10, 25)$ and productions $x = (25, 20, 35)$, what are $(y_1, y_2, y_3)$?
  1. $(25, 45, 80)$
  2. $(26, 26, 51)$
  3. $(5, 10, 10)$
  4. $(6, 16, 26)$
Explanation. $y_t = y_{t-1} + x_t - d_t$, starting from $y_0 = 1$: we get $(6, 16, 26)$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-mod-scorte · level facile · notes
In a multi-period model with $3$ periods, $x_t$ is the production and $y_t$ the inventory at the end of period $t$, with initial inventory $y_0 = 0$ and balance $y_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + y_t$. With demands $d = (15, 40, 10)$ and productions $x = (45, 40, 40)$, what are $(y_1, y_2, y_3)$?

  2. $(45, 70, 70)$
  3. $(30, 30, 60)$
  4. $(30, 0, 30)$
Explanation. $y_t = y_{t-1} + x_t - d_t$, starting from $y_0 = 0$: we get $(30, 30, 60)$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-mod-assoluti · level media · notes
We want $\min\ |x - 11| + |x - 12| + |x - 16|$ with real $x$, linearizing each term with $w_i \ge x - a_i$, $w_i \ge a_i - x$ and $\min\ w_1 + w_2 + w_3$. What is the optimal value of the LP model?
  1. $0$
  2. $6$
  3. $5$
  4. $4$
Explanation. At the optimum $w_i = |x - a_i|$; the sum of distances is minimal at the median $x = 12$: value $5$ (at the mean $x = 13$ it is $6$).

  4. $10$
Explanation. At the optimum $w_i = |x - a_i|$; the sum of distances is minimal at the median $x = 8$: value $17$ (at the mean $x = 9$ it is $18$).
Example 1 · lpm-g-mod-trasporto · level complessa · notes
Balanced transportation problem: two warehouses with availability $o = (25, 30)$, two clients with demands $d = (35, 20)$, unit costs $k_{11} = 8$, $k_{12} = 1$, $k_{21} = 8$, $k_{22} = 6$, variables $x_{ij} \ge 0$ and constraints $\sum_j x_{ij} = o_i$, $\sum_i x_{ij} = d_j$. What is the minimum transportation cost?
  1. $400$
  2. $300$
  3. $305$
  4. $350$
Explanation. With $x_{11} = t$ the other variables are determined and $t \in [5, 25]$; the cost is linear in $t$, so the minimum is at an endpoint: $300$.
