5. Multi-period production and inventory (3)

Example 1 · lpm-g-prod-2mesi-costo · level facile · notes
Production-inventory LP, one product, two months: $x_t$ = units produced and $s_t$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, balance $s_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + s_t$ ($s_0 = 0$), capacity $x_t \le b_t$; minimize $\sum_t (c\,x_t + h\,s_t)$. Data: demand $d_1 = 55$, $d_2 = 100$; $b_1 = b_2 = 80$ units; $c = 14$ €/unit, holding cost $h = 1$ €/unit/month. What is the optimal cost of the plan?
  1. 2190 €
  2. 2270 €
  3. 2170 €
  4. 2210 €
Explanation. Month 2 produces at most $80$, so $\tilde s_1 = d_2 - b_2 = 20$ units are produced in month 1 and held for one month: $14\cdot 155 + 1\cdot 20 = 2190$ €.
Example 2 · lpm-g-prod-2mesi-costo · level facile · notes
Production-inventory LP, one product, two months: $x_t$ = units produced and $s_t$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, balance $s_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + s_t$ ($s_0 = 0$), capacity $x_t \le b_t$; minimize $\sum_t (c\,x_t + h\,s_t)$. Data: demand $d_1 = 25$, $d_2 = 100$; $b_1 = b_2 = 70$ units; $c = 6$ €/unit, holding cost $h = 2$ €/unit/month. What is the optimal cost of the plan?
  1. 810 €
  2. 750 €
  3. 950 €
  4. 870 €
Explanation. Month 2 produces at most $70$, so $\tilde s_1 = d_2 - b_2 = 30$ units are produced in month 1 and held for one month: $6\cdot 125 + 2\cdot 30 = 810$ €.
Example 1 · lpm-g-prod-2mesi-duali · level facile · notes
Production-inventory LP, one product, two months: $x_t$ = units produced and $s_t$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, balance $s_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + s_t$ ($s_0 = 0$), capacity $x_t \le b_t$; minimize $\sum_t (c\,x_t + h\,s_t)$. Data: demand $d_1 = 50$, $d_2 = 100$; $b_1 = b_2 = 80$ units; $c = 14$ €/unit, holding cost $h = 1$ €/unit/month. What are the shadow prices $\tilde\pi_t = \partial\tilde z/\partial b_t$ of the two capacity constraints ($\tilde z$ = optimal cost; Gurobi's sign convention)?
  1. $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$, $\tilde\pi_2 = -1$
  2. $\tilde\pi_1 = -1$, $\tilde\pi_2 = 0$
  3. $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$, $\tilde\pi_2 = 1$
  4. $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$, $\tilde\pi_2 = -14$
Explanation. One more unit of capacity in month 2 lets one unit be produced a month later, saving the holding cost: $\tilde\pi_2 = -h = -1$ (a $\le$ constraint in a minimization has $\tilde\pi \le 0$). Month 1 produces $70 < 80$: its constraint is slack and $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-prod-2mesi-duali · level facile · notes
Production-inventory LP, one product, two months: $x_t$ = units produced and $s_t$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, balance $s_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + s_t$ ($s_0 = 0$), capacity $x_t \le b_t$; minimize $\sum_t (c\,x_t + h\,s_t)$. Data: demand $d_1 = 25$, $d_2 = 100$; $b_1 = b_2 = 70$ units; $c = 6$ €/unit, holding cost $h = 2$ €/unit/month. What are the shadow prices $\tilde\pi_t = \partial\tilde z/\partial b_t$ of the two capacity constraints ($\tilde z$ = optimal cost; Gurobi's sign convention)?
  1. $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$, $\tilde\pi_2 = -2$
  2. $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$, $\tilde\pi_2 = 2$
  3. $\tilde\pi_1 = -2$, $\tilde\pi_2 = 0$
  4. $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$, $\tilde\pi_2 = -6$
Explanation. One more unit of capacity in month 2 lets one unit be produced a month later, saving the holding cost: $\tilde\pi_2 = -h = -2$ (a $\le$ constraint in a minimization has $\tilde\pi \le 0$). Month 1 produces $55 < 70$: its constraint is slack and $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-prod-3mesi · level complessa · notes
Production-inventory LP, one product, three months: $x_t$ = units produced and $s_t$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, balance $s_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + s_t$ ($s_0 = 0$), capacity $x_t \le b_t$; minimize $z = \sum_t (c\,x_t + h\,s_t)$. Data: $(d_1, d_2, d_3) = (60, 55, 120)$, $(b_1, b_2, b_3) = (80, 80, 90)$, $c = 9$ €/unit, $h = 2$ €/unit/month. What are the optimal cost $\tilde z$ and the optimal inventories $\tilde s_1, \tilde s_2$?
  1. $\tilde z = 2245$ €, $\tilde s_1 = 20$, $\tilde s_2 = 45$
  2. $\tilde z = 2175$ €, $\tilde s_1 = 0$, $\tilde s_2 = 30$
  3. $\tilde z = 2185$ €, $\tilde s_1 = 5$, $\tilde s_2 = 30$
  4. $\tilde z = 2175$ €, $\tilde s_1 = 5$, $\tilde s_2 = 30$
Explanation. With the same $c$ every month it pays to produce as late as possible. Month 3 lacks $120 - 90 = 30$ units: $\tilde s_2 = 30$. Month 2 must cover $55 + 30 = 85 > 80$, so $\tilde s_1 = 5$. Cost $9\cdot 235 + 2\cdot(5 + 30) = 2185$ €.
Example 2 · lpm-g-prod-3mesi · level complessa · notes
Production-inventory LP, one product, three months: $x_t$ = units produced and $s_t$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, balance $s_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + s_t$ ($s_0 = 0$), capacity $x_t \le b_t$; minimize $z = \sum_t (c\,x_t + h\,s_t)$. Data: $(d_1, d_2, d_3) = (50, 100, 70)$, $(b_1, b_2, b_3) = (70, 100, 60)$, $c = 11$ €/unit, $h = 2$ €/unit/month. What are the optimal cost $\tilde z$ and the optimal inventories $\tilde s_1, \tilde s_2$?
  1. $\tilde z = 2460$ €, $\tilde s_1 = 10$, $\tilde s_2 = 10$
  2. $\tilde z = 2440$ €, $\tilde s_1 = 0$, $\tilde s_2 = 10$
  3. $\tilde z = 2500$ €, $\tilde s_1 = 20$, $\tilde s_2 = 20$
  4. $\tilde z = 2440$ €, $\tilde s_1 = 10$, $\tilde s_2 = 10$
Explanation. With the same $c$ every month it pays to produce as late as possible. Month 3 lacks $70 - 60 = 10$ units: $\tilde s_2 = 10$. Month 2 must cover $100 + 10 = 110 > 100$, so $\tilde s_1 = 10$. Cost $11\cdot 220 + 2\cdot(10 + 10) = 2460$ €.

6. Smart charging of electric vehicles (2)

Example 1 · lpm-g-ev-un-veicolo · level facile · notes
EV charging LP, one vehicle, two one-hour slots: $x_t$ = energy charged in hour $t$ (kWh), $0 \le x_t \le \bar p = 11$ (charger power, kW), requirement $x_1 + x_2 \ge e = 20$ kWh (efficiency $r = 1$); minimize $c_1x_1 + c_2x_2$ with $c_1 = 0.15$, $c_2 = 0.25$ €/kWh. What are the minimum cost and the shadow price of the requirement (cost of one more kWh)?
  1. cost 3.9 €, shadow price 0.1 €/kWh
  2. cost 3.9 €, shadow price 0.15 €/kWh
  3. cost 3 €, shadow price 0.15 €/kWh
  4. cost 3.9 €, shadow price 0.25 €/kWh
Explanation. The cheap hour is used at full power ($11$ kWh), the remaining $9$ kWh go into hour 2: $3.9$ €. One more kWh would end up in the expensive hour: the dual is the price of the marginal hour, $0.25$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-ev-un-veicolo · level facile · notes
EV charging LP, one vehicle, two one-hour slots: $x_t$ = energy charged in hour $t$ (kWh), $0 \le x_t \le \bar p = 6$ (charger power, kW), requirement $x_1 + x_2 \ge e = 7$ kWh (efficiency $r = 1$); minimize $c_1x_1 + c_2x_2$ with $c_1 = 0.12$, $c_2 = 0.3$ €/kWh. What are the minimum cost and the shadow price of the requirement (cost of one more kWh)?
  1. cost 1.02 €, shadow price 0.3 €/kWh
  2. cost 1.02 €, shadow price 0.18 €/kWh
  3. cost 0.84 €, shadow price 0.12 €/kWh
  4. cost 1.02 €, shadow price 0.12 €/kWh
Explanation. The cheap hour is used at full power ($6$ kWh), the remaining $1$ kWh go into hour 2: $1.02$ €. One more kWh would end up in the expensive hour: the dual is the price of the marginal hour, $0.3$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-ev-tre-ore · level media · notes
EV charging LP, one vehicle plugged in for three one-hour slots: $x_t$ = energy drawn from the grid in hour $t$ (kWh), $0 \le x_t \le \bar p = 9$; requirement $r\,(x_1 + x_2 + x_3) \ge e = 18$ kWh, with efficiency $r = 0.9$; minimize $\sum_t c_t x_t$ with $(c_1, c_2, c_3) = (0.18, 0.25, 0.08)$ €/kWh. What are the minimum cost and the shadow price of the requirement (cost of one more kWh in the battery)?
  1. cost 2.34 €, shadow price 0.18 €/kWh
  2. cost 2.84 €, shadow price 0.25 €/kWh
  3. cost 2.84 €, shadow price 0.278 €/kWh
  4. cost 2.84 €, shadow price 0.089 €/kWh
Explanation. The grid must supply $e/r = 20$ kWh, bought in the cheapest hours at full power $\bar p$: cost $2.84$ €. One more kWh in the battery needs $1/r$ kWh in the marginal hour (price $0.25$): shadow price $0.25/0.9 = 0.278$ €/kWh. Forgetting $r$ buys only $e$ kWh.
Example 2 · lpm-g-ev-tre-ore · level media · notes
EV charging LP, one vehicle plugged in for three one-hour slots: $x_t$ = energy drawn from the grid in hour $t$ (kWh), $0 \le x_t \le \bar p = 9$; requirement $r\,(x_1 + x_2 + x_3) \ge e = 21.6$ kWh, with efficiency $r = 0.9$; minimize $\sum_t c_t x_t$ with $(c_1, c_2, c_3) = (0.25, 0.2, 0.3)$ €/kWh. What are the minimum cost and the shadow price of the requirement (cost of one more kWh in the battery)?
  1. cost 5.13 €, shadow price 0.3 €/kWh
  2. cost 5.85 €, shadow price 0.3 €/kWh
  3. cost 5.85 €, shadow price 0.333 €/kWh
  4. cost 5.85 €, shadow price 0.222 €/kWh
Explanation. The grid must supply $e/r = 24$ kWh, bought in the cheapest hours at full power $\bar p$: cost $5.85$ €. One more kWh in the battery needs $1/r$ kWh in the marginal hour (price $0.3$): shadow price $0.3/0.9 = 0.333$ €/kWh. Forgetting $r$ buys only $e$ kWh.

7. Data Envelopment Analysis (3)

Example 1 · lpm-g-dea-ccr-1x1 · level facile · notes
DEA with five DMUs (decision-making units), one input $x$ and one output $y$. DMU $j$: 1 $(x_{11}, y_{11}) = (8, 7)$; 2 $(x_{12}, y_{12}) = (10, 11)$; 3 $(x_{13}, y_{13}) = (3, 10)$; 4 $(x_{14}, y_{14}) = (8, 8)$; 5 $(x_{15}, y_{15}) = (11, 1)$. The CCR efficiency of DMU $o$ is $h_o = \max\, u\,y_o/(v\,x_o)$ over the weights $u, v \ge 0$ that give every DMU $j$ a ratio $u\,y_j/(v\,x_j) \le 1$. What is the CCR efficiency $\tilde h_{5}$ of DMU 5?
  1. $\tilde h_{5} = 0.083$
  2. $\tilde h_{5} = 0.091$
  3. $\tilde h_{5} = 0.273$
  4. $\tilde h_{5} = 0.027$
Explanation. With one input and one output the weights cancel: the efficiency is $y_o/x_o$ divided by the best ratio, $10/3$ of DMU 3: $(1/11)/(10/3) = 0.027$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-dea-ccr-1x1 · level facile · notes
DEA with five DMUs (decision-making units), one input $x$ and one output $y$. DMU $j$: 1 $(x_{11}, y_{11}) = (6, 11)$; 2 $(x_{12}, y_{12}) = (5, 2)$; 3 $(x_{13}, y_{13}) = (6, 11)$; 4 $(x_{14}, y_{14}) = (10, 8)$; 5 $(x_{15}, y_{15}) = (12, 10)$. The CCR efficiency of DMU $o$ is $h_o = \max\, u\,y_o/(v\,x_o)$ over the weights $u, v \ge 0$ that give every DMU $j$ a ratio $u\,y_j/(v\,x_j) \le 1$. What is the CCR efficiency $\tilde h_{4}$ of DMU 4?
  1. $\tilde h_{4} = 0.727$
  2. $\tilde h_{4} = 0.436$
  3. $\tilde h_{4} = 0.5$
  4. $\tilde h_{4} = 0.8$
Explanation. With one input and one output the weights cancel: the efficiency is $y_o/x_o$ divided by the best ratio, $11/6$ of DMU 1: $(8/10)/(11/6) = 0.436$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-dea-bcc-1x1 · level media · notes
DEA with five DMUs (decision-making units), one input $x$ and one output $y$. DMU $j$: 1 $(x_{11}, y_{11}) = (4, 4)$; 2 $(x_{12}, y_{12}) = (2, 2)$; 3 $(x_{13}, y_{13}) = (5, 7)$; 4 $(x_{14}, y_{14}) = (3, 6)$; 5 $(x_{15}, y_{15}) = (7, 9)$. The input-oriented BCC efficiency $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC}$ of DMU $o$ is the smallest $\theta$ such that a convex combination of the DMUs ($\lambda_j \ge 0$, $\sum_j\lambda_j = 1$) produces at least $y_o$ with at most $\theta x_o$; the scale efficiency is $\mathrm{SE} = \tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}/\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC}$. DMU 1 has $\tilde\theta^{\rm CCR} = 0.5$. What are its $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC}$ and $\mathrm{SE}$?
  1. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.625$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.5$
  2. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.75$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.667$
  3. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.625$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.8$
  4. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.5$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.8$
Explanation. The BCC virtual DMU is a convex combination ($\sum_j \lambda_j = 1$) producing at least $y_o = 4$ with the least input: combining two DMUs on either side of $y_o$ gives $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.625$, better than the best single DMU ($0.75$). Then $\mathrm{SE} = \tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}/\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.8$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-dea-bcc-1x1 · level media · notes
DEA with five DMUs (decision-making units), one input $x$ and one output $y$. DMU $j$: 1 $(x_{11}, y_{11}) = (6, 11)$; 2 $(x_{12}, y_{12}) = (5, 2)$; 3 $(x_{13}, y_{13}) = (6, 11)$; 4 $(x_{14}, y_{14}) = (10, 8)$; 5 $(x_{15}, y_{15}) = (12, 10)$. The input-oriented BCC efficiency $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC}$ of DMU $o$ is the smallest $\theta$ such that a convex combination of the DMUs ($\lambda_j \ge 0$, $\sum_j\lambda_j = 1$) produces at least $y_o$ with at most $\theta x_o$; the scale efficiency is $\mathrm{SE} = \tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}/\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC}$. DMU 4 has $\tilde\theta^{\rm CCR} = 0.436$. What are its $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC}$ and $\mathrm{SE}$?
  1. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.6$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.727$
  2. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.567$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.77$
  3. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.436$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.77$
  4. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.567$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.436$
Explanation. The BCC virtual DMU is a convex combination ($\sum_j \lambda_j = 1$) producing at least $y_o = 8$ with the least input: combining two DMUs on either side of $y_o$ gives $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.567$, better than the best single DMU ($0.6$). Then $\mathrm{SE} = \tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}/\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.77$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-dea-ccr-2input · level complessa · notes
DEA with five DMUs (decision-making units), two inputs and one output, data $(x_{1j}, x_{2j}; y_{1j})$: 1: $(24, 4; 2)$, 2: $(18, 16; 2)$, 3: $(4, 16; 2)$, 4: $(30, 36; 3)$, 5: $(30, 30; 3)$. Input-oriented CCR envelopment model for DMU $o$: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$ ($i = 1, 2$), $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{1j} \ge y_{1o}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$. For $o = 4$, what are the efficiency $\tilde\theta$ and the radial input targets $(\tilde\theta x_{1o}; \tilde\theta x_{2o})$?
  1. $\tilde\theta = 0.167$, target $(5; 6)$
  2. $\tilde\theta = 0.341$, target $(10.22; 12.27)$
  3. $\tilde\theta = 0.511$, target $(15.33; 18.4)$
  4. $\tilde\theta = 0.667$, target $(20; 24)$
Explanation. With constant returns divide the inputs by the output: the points $(x_{1j}/y_{1j}, x_{2j}/y_{2j})$ are compared with the same output. The envelopment form looks for the smallest $\theta$ such that a combination of DMUs uses at most $\theta$ times both inputs of DMU 4: here the optimum combines two DMUs, $\tilde\theta = 0.511$ (the best single DMU gives only $0.667$).
Example 2 · lpm-g-dea-ccr-2input · level complessa · notes
DEA with five DMUs (decision-making units), two inputs and one output, data $(x_{1j}, x_{2j}; y_{1j})$: 1: $(5, 8; 1)$, 2: $(22, 22; 2)$, 3: $(7, 11; 1)$, 4: $(20, 18; 2)$, 5: $(10, 5; 1)$. Input-oriented CCR envelopment model for DMU $o$: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$ ($i = 1, 2$), $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{1j} \ge y_{1o}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$. For $o = 2$, what are the efficiency $\tilde\theta$ and the radial input targets $(\tilde\theta x_{1o}; \tilde\theta x_{2o})$?
  1. $\tilde\theta = 0.313$, target $(6.88; 6.88)$
  2. $\tilde\theta = 0.727$, target $(16; 16)$
  3. $\tilde\theta = 0.625$, target $(13.75; 13.75)$
  4. $\tilde\theta = 0.455$, target $(10; 10)$
Explanation. With constant returns divide the inputs by the output: the points $(x_{1j}/y_{1j}, x_{2j}/y_{2j})$ are compared with the same output. The envelopment form looks for the smallest $\theta$ such that a combination of DMUs uses at most $\theta$ times both inputs of DMU 2: here the optimum combines two DMUs, $\tilde\theta = 0.625$ (the best single DMU gives only $0.727$).

8. The Newsvendor and its variants (5)

Example 1 · lpm-g-nv-uniforme · level facile · notes
Newsvendor: a quantity $q$ is ordered before the demand is known; each unit costs $c = 12$ €, sells at $p = 21$ € and, if unsold, is salvaged at $v = 9$ €. Demand is uniform on $[140, 164]$ and $q$ maximizes the expected profit. What are the critical ratio $f^* = \mathbb P(D \le q^*)$ and the optimal quantity $q^*$?
  1. $f^* = 0.75$, $q^* = 158$
  2. $f^* = 0.5$, $q^* = 152$
  3. $f^* = 0.25$, $q^* = 146$
  4. $f^* = 0.6364$, $q^* = 155.3$
Explanation. $c_u = p - c = 9$, $c_o = c - v = 3$, $f^* = c_u/(c_u + c_o) = 9/12$; for the uniform distribution $F^{-1}(f) = 140 + 24\,f$, so $q^* = 158$, not the mean.
Example 2 · lpm-g-nv-uniforme · level facile · notes
Newsvendor: a quantity $q$ is ordered before the demand is known; each unit costs $c = 14$ €, sells at $p = 18$ € and, if unsold, is salvaged at $v = 4$ €. Demand is uniform on $[60, 116]$ and $q$ maximizes the expected profit. What are the critical ratio $f^* = \mathbb P(D \le q^*)$ and the optimal quantity $q^*$?
  1. $f^* = 0.2857$, $q^* = 76$
  2. $f^* = 0.7143$, $q^* = 100$
  3. $f^* = 0.5$, $q^* = 88$
  4. $f^* = 0.5625$, $q^* = 91.5$
Explanation. $c_u = p - c = 4$, $c_o = c - v = 10$, $f^* = c_u/(c_u + c_o) = 4/14$; for the uniform distribution $F^{-1}(f) = 60 + 56\,f$, so $q^* = 76$, not the mean.
Example 1 · lpm-g-nv-discreto · level media · notes
Newsvendor: a quantity $q$ is ordered before the demand $D$ is known, to minimize the expected cost $\mathbb E[c_o(q - D)^+ + c_u(D - q)^+]$, with underage cost $c_u = 4$ per unit of unmet demand and overage cost $c_o = 12$ per unsold unit. Demand scenarios (probability in brackets): $70$ (0.3), $110$ (0.1), $120$ (0.3), $130$ (0.3). What are the optimal quantity $q^*$ and its expected cost?
  1. $q^* = 130$, expected cost 276
  2. $q^* = 70$, expected cost 148
  3. $q^* = 110$, expected cost 180
  4. $q^* = 120$, expected cost 204
Explanation. $f^* = 4/16 = 0.25$: the optimum is the smallest $q$ with $F(q) \ge f^*$, that is $q^* = 70$, with expected cost $\sum_s w_s (c_o (q - d_s)^+ + c_u (d_s - q)^+) = 148$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-nv-discreto · level media · notes
Newsvendor: a quantity $q$ is ordered before the demand $D$ is known, to minimize the expected cost $\mathbb E[c_o(q - D)^+ + c_u(D - q)^+]$, with underage cost $c_u = 11$ per unit of unmet demand and overage cost $c_o = 4$ per unsold unit. Demand scenarios (probability in brackets): $60$ (0.4), $100$ (0.3), $110$ (0.1), $130$ (0.1), $140$ (0.1). What are the optimal quantity $q^*$ and its expected cost?
  1. $q^* = 110$, expected cost 147
  2. $q^* = 100$, expected cost 152
  3. $q^* = 60$, expected cost 352
  4. $q^* = 130$, expected cost 167
Explanation. $f^* = 11/15 = 0.7333$: the optimum is the smallest $q$ with $F(q) \ge f^*$, that is $q^* = 110$, with expected cost $\sum_s w_s (c_o (q - d_s)^+ + c_u (d_s - q)^+) = 147$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-nv-vss · level complessa · notes
Newsvendor: a quantity $q$ is ordered before the demand $D$ is known, to minimize the expected cost $\mathbb E[c_o(q - D)^+ + c_u(D - q)^+]$, with underage cost $c_u = 7$ per unit of unmet demand and overage cost $c_o = 11$ per unsold unit. Demand scenarios (probability in brackets): $130$ (0.4), $140$ (0.2), $160$ (0.2), $190$ (0.1), $200$ (0.1). Compute the optimal quantity $q^*$, its expected cost and the value of the stochastic solution VSS (expected cost of ordering the mean demand minus the cost of $q^*$).
  1. $q^* = 130$, cost 147, VSS $= 147$
  2. $q^* = 140$, cost 149, VSS $= 41.8$
  3. $q^* = 130$, cost 147, VSS $= 190.8$
  4. $q^* = 130$, cost 147, VSS $= 43.8$
Explanation. $f^* = 0.3889$ gives $q^* = 130$ with cost $147$. The mean demand is $151$, whose expected cost is $190.8$: VSS $= 190.8 - 147 = 43.8$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-nv-vss · level complessa · notes
Newsvendor: a quantity $q$ is ordered before the demand $D$ is known, to minimize the expected cost $\mathbb E[c_o(q - D)^+ + c_u(D - q)^+]$, with underage cost $c_u = 11$ per unit of unmet demand and overage cost $c_o = 4$ per unsold unit. Demand scenarios (probability in brackets): $60$ (0.4), $100$ (0.3), $110$ (0.1), $130$ (0.1), $140$ (0.1). Compute the optimal quantity $q^*$, its expected cost and the value of the stochastic solution VSS (expected cost of ordering the mean demand minus the cost of $q^*$).
  1. $q^* = 110$, cost 147, VSS $= 45$
  2. $q^* = 110$, cost 147, VSS $= 192$
  3. $q^* = 100$, cost 152, VSS $= 40$
  4. $q^* = 110$, cost 147, VSS $= 147$
Explanation. $f^* = 0.7333$ gives $q^* = 110$ with cost $147$. The mean demand is $92$, whose expected cost is $192$: VSS $= 192 - 147 = 45$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-nv-normale · level facile · notes
Newsvendor: a product is bought at $c = 8$ €, sold at $p = 20$ € and each unsold unit is recovered at $v = 4$ €; demand is normal with mean $190$ and standard deviation $10$. The optimal order satisfies $F(\tilde q) = c_u/(c_u + c_o)$ with $c_u = p - c$ and $c_o = c - v$, so $\tilde q = \mu + z\sigma$ with $z = \Phi^{-1}(c_u/(c_u + c_o))$ (standard normal quantiles: $\Phi^{-1}(0.6) = 0.253$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.7) = 0.524$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.75) = 0.674$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.8) = 0.842$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.9) = 1.282$). What is $\tilde q$?
  1. $\tilde q = 196.7$
  2. $\tilde q = 198.4$
  3. $\tilde q = 183.3$
  4. $\tilde q = 190$
Explanation. $c_u = 12$, $c_o = 4$, critical ratio $12/16 = 0.75$, $z = 0.674$: $\tilde q = 190 + 0.674\cdot 10 = 196.7$, above the mean because shortages cost more than leftovers. Swapping $c_u$ and $c_o$ gives $\mu - z\sigma$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-nv-normale · level facile · notes
Newsvendor: a product is bought at $c = 8$ €, sold at $p = 22$ € and each unsold unit is recovered at $v = 2$ €; demand is normal with mean $90$ and standard deviation $20$. The optimal order satisfies $F(\tilde q) = c_u/(c_u + c_o)$ with $c_u = p - c$ and $c_o = c - v$, so $\tilde q = \mu + z\sigma$ with $z = \Phi^{-1}(c_u/(c_u + c_o))$ (standard normal quantiles: $\Phi^{-1}(0.6) = 0.253$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.7) = 0.524$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.75) = 0.674$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.8) = 0.842$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.9) = 1.282$). What is $\tilde q$?
  1. $\tilde q = 100.5$
  2. $\tilde q = 79.5$
  3. $\tilde q = 103.5$
  4. $\tilde q = 90$
Explanation. $c_u = 14$, $c_o = 6$, critical ratio $14/20 = 0.7$, $z = 0.524$: $\tilde q = 90 + 0.524\cdot 20 = 100.5$, above the mean because shortages cost more than leftovers. Swapping $c_u$ and $c_o$ gives $\mu - z\sigma$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-nv-servizio-uniforme · level media · notes
Newsvendor with uniform demand on $[90, 180]$, $c_u = 6$, $c_o = 9$. The expected cost of an order $q$ in $[90, 180]$ is $C(q) = \frac{c_o(q - 90)^2 + c_u(180 - q)^2}{2\cdot 90}$, minimized at $\tilde q = 90 + \frac{c_u}{c_u + c_o}\cdot 90$. A contract imposes a cycle service level $\mathbb P(D \le q) = 0.9$. What are the required order $q_\beta$ and its extra expected cost $C(q_\beta) - C(\tilde q)$?
  1. $q_\beta = 171$, extra cost $33.75$
  2. $q_\beta = 99$, extra cost $168.75$
  3. $q_\beta = 171$, extra cost $168.75$
  4. $q_\beta = 171$, extra cost $330.75$
Explanation. $q_\beta = 90 + 0.9\cdot 90 = 171$; $\tilde q = 126$; $C(q_\beta) = 330.75$ and $C(\tilde q) = 162$, so the service level costs $168.75$ more per cycle than the economic optimum, whose service level is only $0.4$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-nv-servizio-uniforme · level media · notes
Newsvendor with uniform demand on $[70, 180]$, $c_u = 4$, $c_o = 2$. The expected cost of an order $q$ in $[70, 180]$ is $C(q) = \frac{c_o(q - 70)^2 + c_u(180 - q)^2}{2\cdot 110}$, minimized at $\tilde q = 70 + \frac{c_u}{c_u + c_o}\cdot 110$. A contract imposes a cycle service level $\mathbb P(D \le q) = 0.9$. What are the required order $q_\beta$ and its extra expected cost $C(q_\beta) - C(\tilde q)$?
  1. $q_\beta = 169$, extra cost $17.97$
  2. $q_\beta = 169$, extra cost $69.3$
  3. $q_\beta = 81$, extra cost $17.97$
  4. $q_\beta = 169$, extra cost $91.3$
Explanation. $q_\beta = 70 + 0.9\cdot 110 = 169$; $\tilde q = 143.33$; $C(q_\beta) = 91.3$ and $C(\tilde q) = 73.33$, so the service level costs $17.97$ more per cycle than the economic optimum, whose service level is only $0.67$.

9. VaR and CVaR (4)

Example 1 · lpm-g-cvar-coda-intera · level facile · notes
Risk measures of a loss $L$ with $k = 10$ equally likely scenarios: $24, 32, 35, 5, 33, 25, 36, 1, 8, 11$. $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ is the smallest loss $\ell$ with $\mathbb P(L \le \ell) \ge \alpha$; $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$ is the mean loss in the worst tail of probability $1 - \alpha$. With $\alpha = 0.7$, what are $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$?
  1. VaR $= 33$, CVaR $= 34.67$
  2. VaR $= 32$, CVaR $= 34$
  3. VaR $= 32$, CVaR $= 36$
  4. VaR $= 32$, CVaR $= 34.67$
Explanation. Sorting the losses, the distribution function reaches $0.7$ at position 7 in increasing order: VaR $= 32$. The tail of mass $1 - \alpha$ contains exactly the 3 worst losses, whose mean is the CVaR, $34.67$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-cvar-coda-intera · level facile · notes
Risk measures of a loss $L$ with $k = 8$ equally likely scenarios: $34, 13, 4, 16, 35, 32, 27, 38$. $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ is the smallest loss $\ell$ with $\mathbb P(L \le \ell) \ge \alpha$; $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$ is the mean loss in the worst tail of probability $1 - \alpha$. With $\alpha = 0.75$, what are $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$?
  1. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 36.5$
  2. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 35.67$
  3. VaR $= 35$, CVaR $= 36.5$
  4. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 38$
Explanation. Sorting the losses, the distribution function reaches $0.75$ at position 6 in increasing order: VaR $= 34$. The tail of mass $1 - \alpha$ contains exactly the 2 worst losses, whose mean is the CVaR, $36.5$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-cvar-a-cavallo · level media · notes
Risk measures of a loss $L$ with six equally likely scenarios: $24, 32, 35, 5, 33, 25$. $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ is the smallest loss $\ell$ with $\mathbb P(L \le \ell) \ge \alpha$, and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha = \mathrm{VaR}_\alpha + \mathbb E[(L - \mathrm{VaR}_\alpha)^+]/(1 - \alpha)$. With $\alpha = 0.75$ (the tail of probability $1 - \alpha$ is not a whole number of scenarios), what are $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$?
  1. VaR $= 33$, CVaR $= 34$
  2. VaR $= 33$, CVaR $= 34.33$
  3. VaR $= 35$, CVaR $= 34.33$
  4. VaR $= 33$, CVaR $= 35$
Explanation. VaR $= 33$, the first loss at which the distribution function reaches $\alpha$. Then $\mathrm{CVaR} = \mathrm{VaR} + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s w_s (\ell_s - \mathrm{VaR})^+ = 34.33$: the part of the tail mass that sits on the VaR point is counted at the VaR value.
Example 2 · lpm-g-cvar-a-cavallo · level media · notes
Risk measures of a loss $L$ with six equally likely scenarios: $34, 13, 4, 16, 35, 32$. $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ is the smallest loss $\ell$ with $\mathbb P(L \le \ell) \ge \alpha$, and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha = \mathrm{VaR}_\alpha + \mathbb E[(L - \mathrm{VaR}_\alpha)^+]/(1 - \alpha)$. With $\alpha = 0.75$ (the tail of probability $1 - \alpha$ is not a whole number of scenarios), what are $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$?
  1. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 34.67$
  2. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 34.5$
  3. VaR $= 35$, CVaR $= 34.67$
  4. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 35$
Explanation. VaR $= 34$, the first loss at which the distribution function reaches $\alpha$. Then $\mathrm{CVaR} = \mathrm{VaR} + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s w_s (\ell_s - \mathrm{VaR})^+ = 34.67$: the part of the tail mass that sits on the VaR point is counted at the VaR value.
Example 1 · lpm-g-cvar-pesato · level complessa · notes
Risk measures of a loss $L$ with scenarios (probability in brackets): $26$ (0.3), $20$ (0.3), $9$ (0.3), $3$ (0.05), $27$ (0.05). $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ is the smallest loss $\ell$ with $\mathbb P(L \le \ell) \ge \alpha$; $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$ is the optimal value of the Rockafellar–Uryasev LP $\min_\eta\, \eta + \mathbb E[(L - \eta)^+]/(1 - \alpha)$. With $\alpha = 0.9$, what are $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$?
  1. VaR $= 26$, CVaR $= 26.14$
  2. VaR $= 26$, CVaR $= 27$
  3. VaR $= 27$, CVaR $= 27$
  4. VaR $= 26$, CVaR $= 26.5$
Explanation. Sorting the losses and accumulating the probabilities, $F$ reaches $0.9$ at $\ell = 26$: VaR $= 26$. Then $\mathrm{CVaR} = 26 + \frac{1}{0.1}\sum_s w_s(\ell_s - 26)^+ = 26.5$. The conditional mean beyond the VaR ignores the tail mass sitting on the VaR point.
Example 2 · lpm-g-cvar-pesato · level complessa · notes
Risk measures of a loss $L$ with scenarios (probability in brackets): $34$ (0.35), $13$ (0.3), $4$ (0.25), $16$ (0.05), $35$ (0.05). $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ is the smallest loss $\ell$ with $\mathbb P(L \le \ell) \ge \alpha$; $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$ is the optimal value of the Rockafellar–Uryasev LP $\min_\eta\, \eta + \mathbb E[(L - \eta)^+]/(1 - \alpha)$. With $\alpha = 0.85$, what are $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$?
  1. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 34.33$
  2. VaR $= 35$, CVaR $= 35$
  3. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 34.13$
  4. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 35$
Explanation. Sorting the losses and accumulating the probabilities, $F$ reaches $0.85$ at $\ell = 34$: VaR $= 34$. Then $\mathrm{CVaR} = 34 + \frac{1}{0.15}\sum_s w_s(\ell_s - 34)^+ = 34.33$. The conditional mean beyond the VaR ignores the tail mass sitting on the VaR point.
Example 1 · lpm-g-cvar-eccessi · level facile · notes
Rockafellar–Uryasev formulation of the CVaR: for a threshold $\eta$, the excesses are $\xi_s = (\ell_s - \eta)^+$ and the function to minimize over $\eta$ is $F(\eta) = \eta + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s w_s\xi_s$. Five equiprobable scenarios ($w_s = 0.2$) with losses $\ell = (18; 18; 24; 26; 4)$, $\alpha = 0.8$. For $\eta = 18$ (not necessarily optimal), what are $\sum_s\xi_s$ and $F(\eta)$?
  1. $\sum_s\xi_s = 14$, value $20.8$
  2. $\sum_s\xi_s = 14$, value $32$
  3. $\sum_s\xi_s = 14$, value $21.5$
  4. $\sum_s\xi_s = 0$, value $18$
Explanation. Only the losses above $18$ count: $\xi = (0; 0; 6; 8; 0)$, sum $14$. Then $F(18) = 18 + \frac{1}{0.2}\cdot 0.2\cdot 14 = 32$. Negative differences are cut at zero; forgetting the factor $1/(1-\alpha)$ is the other typical mistake.
Example 2 · lpm-g-cvar-eccessi · level facile · notes
Rockafellar–Uryasev formulation of the CVaR: for a threshold $\eta$, the excesses are $\xi_s = (\ell_s - \eta)^+$ and the function to minimize over $\eta$ is $F(\eta) = \eta + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s w_s\xi_s$. Five equiprobable scenarios ($w_s = 0.2$) with losses $\ell = (12; 26; 10; 3; 12)$, $\alpha = 0.8$. For $\eta = 21$ (not necessarily optimal), what are $\sum_s\xi_s$ and $F(\eta)$?
  1. $\sum_s\xi_s = 5$, value $26$
  2. $\sum_s\xi_s = 5$, value $22$
  3. $\sum_s\xi_s = 5$, value $22.25$
  4. $\sum_s\xi_s = -42$, value $-21$
Explanation. Only the losses above $21$ count: $\xi = (0; 5; 0; 0; 0)$, sum $5$. Then $F(21) = 21 + \frac{1}{0.2}\cdot 0.2\cdot 5 = 26$. Negative differences are cut at zero; forgetting the factor $1/(1-\alpha)$ is the other typical mistake.

10. Arbitrage and pricing (5)

Example 1 · lpm-g-arb-neutrali · level facile · notes
One-period market with two states of the world: a risk-free security costs $1$ today and pays $R = 1.1$ in both states; a risky security costs $s^0_1 = 33$ today and pays $58$ in state 1 and $31$ in state 2. Risk-neutral probabilities are $q_1, q_2 \ge 0$ with $q_1 + q_2 = 1$ under which every price equals the expected payoff divided by $R$. What are $q_1$ and $q_2$?
  1. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.6259; 0.3741)$
  2. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.0741; 0.9259)$
  3. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.8037; 0.1963)$
  4. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.1963; 0.8037)$
Explanation. The price is the discounted expected payoff: $s^0_1 = (q_1 \cdot 58 + q_2 \cdot 31)/R$ with $q_1 + q_2 = 1$, so $q_1 = (R s^0_1 - 31)/(58 - 31) = 0.1963$. Forgetting $R$ is the typical mistake.
Example 2 · lpm-g-arb-neutrali · level facile · notes
One-period market with two states of the world: a risk-free security costs $1$ today and pays $R = 1.05$ in both states; a risky security costs $s^0_1 = 44$ today and pays $50$ in state 1 and $29$ in state 2. Risk-neutral probabilities are $q_1, q_2 \ge 0$ with $q_1 + q_2 = 1$ under which every price equals the expected payoff divided by $R$. What are $q_1$ and $q_2$?
  1. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.819; 0.181)$
  2. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.924; 0.076)$
  3. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.181; 0.819)$
  4. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.7143; 0.2857)$
Explanation. The price is the discounted expected payoff: $s^0_1 = (q_1 \cdot 50 + q_2 \cdot 29)/R$ with $q_1 + q_2 = 1$, so $q_1 = (R s^0_1 - 29)/(50 - 29) = 0.819$. Forgetting $R$ is the typical mistake.
Example 1 · lpm-g-arb-call · level media · notes
One-period market with two states of the world: a risk-free security costs $1$ today and pays $R = 1.1$ in both states; a risky security costs $s^0_1 = 33$ today and pays $58$ in state 1 and $31$ in state 2. Risk-neutral probabilities are $q_1, q_2 \ge 0$ with $q_1 + q_2 = 1$ under which every price equals the expected payoff divided by $R$. A call option with strike $34$ pays tomorrow $\max(s^1_{1j} - 34, 0)$ in state $j$, where $s^1_{1j}$ is the payoff of the risky security. In this complete market, what is the arbitrage-free price of the call today?
  1. 4.28
  2. 1.62
  3. 4.71
  4. 10.91
Explanation. The risk-neutral probabilities are $q_1 = (R s^0_1 - 31)/(58 - 31) = 0.1963$, $q_2 = 1 - q_1$. The call pays $(24, 0)$, so its price is $q_1 \cdot 24/R = 4.28$: discounted expected value under $q$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-arb-call · level media · notes
One-period market with two states of the world: a risk-free security costs $1$ today and pays $R = 1.05$ in both states; a risky security costs $s^0_1 = 44$ today and pays $50$ in state 1 and $29$ in state 2. Risk-neutral probabilities are $q_1, q_2 \ge 0$ with $q_1 + q_2 = 1$ under which every price equals the expected payoff divided by $R$. A call option with strike $37$ pays tomorrow $\max(s^1_{1j} - 37, 0)$ in state $j$, where $s^1_{1j}$ is the payoff of the risky security. In this complete market, what is the arbitrage-free price of the call today?
  1. 10.14
  2. 10.65
  3. 8.84
  4. 6.19
Explanation. The risk-neutral probabilities are $q_1 = (R s^0_1 - 29)/(50 - 29) = 0.819$, $q_2 = 1 - q_1$. The call pays $(13, 0)$, so its price is $q_1 \cdot 13/R = 10.14$: discounted expected value under $q$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-arb-intervallo · level complessa · notes
One-period market with three states: a risk-free security costs $1$ and pays $R = 1.1$ in every state; a risky security costs $s^0_1 = 14$ and pays $(a_1; a_2; a_3) = (5; 36; 11)$ in the three states. A new security pays $(c_1; c_2; c_3) = (5; 1; 2)$. Its price is consistent (creates no arbitrage) if it equals $\frac1R\sum_j q_j\, c_j$ for risk-neutral probabilities $q_j \ge 0$, $\sum_j q_j = 1$, that also price the two existing securities. What is the interval of consistent prices (its endpoints)?
  1. $[1.82; 3.66]$
  2. $[1.71; 3.49]$
  3. $[1.66; 3.33]$
  4. $[0.91; 4.55]$
Explanation. The consistent measures satisfy $\sum_j q_j = 1$ and $\sum_j q_j a_j = R\,s^0_1 = 15.4$: a segment whose endpoints put mass on two states only, one with payoff below and one above $15.4$. Minimizing and maximizing $\frac1R\sum_j q_jc_j$ (two LPs) over it gives $[1.66, 3.33]$. Forgetting the factor $R$ (in the constraint or in the discount) shifts the interval.
Example 2 · lpm-g-arb-intervallo · level complessa · notes
One-period market with three states: a risk-free security costs $1$ and pays $R = 1.05$ in every state; a risky security costs $s^0_1 = 24$ and pays $(a_1; a_2; a_3) = (35; 16; 8)$ in the three states. A new security pays $(c_1; c_2; c_3) = (8; 18; 16)$. Its price is consistent (creates no arbitrage) if it equals $\frac1R\sum_j q_j\, c_j$ for risk-neutral probabilities $q_j \ge 0$, $\sum_j q_j = 1$, that also price the two existing securities. What is the interval of consistent prices (its endpoints)?
  1. $[10.38; 12.53]$
  2. $[10.9; 13.16]$
  3. $[7.62; 17.14]$
  4. $[10.72; 13.13]$
Explanation. The consistent measures satisfy $\sum_j q_j = 1$ and $\sum_j q_j a_j = R\,s^0_1 = 25.2$: a segment whose endpoints put mass on two states only, one with payoff below and one above $25.2$. Minimizing and maximizing $\frac1R\sum_j q_jc_j$ (two LPs) over it gives $[10.38, 12.53]$. Forgetting the factor $R$ (in the constraint or in the discount) shifts the interval.
Example 1 · lpm-g-arb-portafoglio · level facile · notes
One-period market with three states: security 0 is risk-free (costs 1 today, pays $R = 1.05$ in every state); security 1 costs $15$ and pays $(10; 10; 6)$; security 2 costs $25$ and pays $(16; 26; 32)$. The portfolio $x = (-28; -1; -1)$ holds $x_i$ units of security $i$ (negative = short). What are its cost today, $\sum_is^0_ix_i$, and its smallest payoff tomorrow over the three states?
  1. cost $-68$, smallest payoff $-67.4$
  2. cost $-68$, smallest payoff $-66$
  3. cost $-69.4$, smallest payoff $-67.4$
  4. cost $-68$, smallest payoff $-55.4$
Explanation. Cost: $-28\cdot 1 + -1\cdot 15 + -1\cdot 25 = -68$. Payoffs: $-28\cdot 1.05 + -1s^1_{1j} + -1s^1_{2j}$ = $-55.4$, $-65.4$, $-67.4$, smallest $-67.4$. A negative cost with every payoff $\ge 0$ would be an arbitrage of type A. The risk-free position pays $R$ per unit, not 1.
Example 2 · lpm-g-arb-portafoglio · level facile · notes
One-period market with three states: security 0 is risk-free (costs 1 today, pays $R = 1.04$ in every state); security 1 costs $15$ and pays $(18; 9; 6)$; security 2 costs $38$ and pays $(22; 36; 34)$. The portfolio $x = (-36; 2; 1)$ holds $x_i$ units of security $i$ (negative = short). What are its cost today, $\sum_is^0_ix_i$, and its smallest payoff tomorrow over the three states?
  1. cost $32$, smallest payoff $20.56$
  2. cost $32$, smallest payoff $10$
  3. cost $32$, smallest payoff $8.56$
  4. cost $30.56$, smallest payoff $8.56$
Explanation. Cost: $-36\cdot 1 + 2\cdot 15 + 1\cdot 38 = 32$. Payoffs: $-36\cdot 1.04 + 2s^1_{1j} + 1s^1_{2j}$ = $20.56$, $16.56$, $8.56$, smallest $8.56$. A negative cost with every payoff $\ge 0$ would be an arbitrage of type A. The risk-free position pays $R$ per unit, not 1.
Example 1 · lpm-g-arb-prezzo-atteso · level facile · notes
One-period market with three states and risk-free gross return $R = 1.02$. The duals of the arbitrage LP give the risk-neutral probabilities $q = (0.2; 0.1; 0.7)$, and every price today is the discounted expected payoff under $q$: $s^0_i = \frac1R\sum_jq_js^1_{ij}$. A new security pays $(10; 16; 4)$ in the three states. What is its arbitrage-free price today?
  1. $s^0 = 6.27$
  2. $s^0 = 6.53$
  3. $s^0 = 9.8$
  4. $s^0 = 6.4$
Explanation. Expected payoff under $q$: $6.4$; discounted: $6.4/1.02 = 6.27$. Forgetting to discount, or using equal probabilities instead of $q$, are the typical mistakes.
Example 2 · lpm-g-arb-prezzo-atteso · level facile · notes
One-period market with three states and risk-free gross return $R = 1.02$. The duals of the arbitrage LP give the risk-neutral probabilities $q = (0.3; 0.5; 0.2)$, and every price today is the discounted expected payoff under $q$: $s^0_i = \frac1R\sum_jq_js^1_{ij}$. A new security pays $(2; 12; 26)$ in the three states. What is its arbitrage-free price today?
  1. $s^0 = 11.57$
  2. $s^0 = 12.04$
  3. $s^0 = 11.8$
  4. $s^0 = 13.07$
Explanation. Expected payoff under $q$: $11.8$; discounted: $11.8/1.02 = 11.57$. Forgetting to discount, or using equal probabilities instead of $q$, are the typical mistakes.

11. Robust and quantile regression (6)

Example 1 · lpm-g-reg-perdita · level facile · notes
Quantile regression with no features: an intercept $b$ is fitted to $y = (120; 135; 141; 87; 137)$ by minimizing $\sum_i [\tau u_i + (1 - \tau) v_i]$, where $u_i = (y_i - b)^+$ and $v_i = (b - y_i)^+$ are the deviations above and below $b$. With $\tau = 0.8$, what is the objective value for $b = 121$?
  1. $85$
  2. $38$
  3. $47$
  4. $68$
Explanation. The deviations above ($u_i$, observations larger than $b$) sum to $50$ and are paid $\tau$; those below ($v_i$) sum to $35$ and are paid $1 - \tau$: $0.8\cdot 50 + 0.2\cdot 35 = 47$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-reg-perdita · level facile · notes
Quantile regression with no features: an intercept $b$ is fitted to $y = (107; 139; 102; 86; 108)$ by minimizing $\sum_i [\tau u_i + (1 - \tau) v_i]$, where $u_i = (y_i - b)^+$ and $v_i = (b - y_i)^+$ are the deviations above and below $b$. With $\tau = 0.9$, what is the objective value for $b = 130$?
  1. $19.8$
  2. $106.2$
  3. $113.4$
  4. $126$
Explanation. The deviations above ($u_i$, observations larger than $b$) sum to $9$ and are paid $\tau$; those below ($v_i$) sum to $117$ and are paid $1 - \tau$: $0.9\cdot 9 + 0.1\cdot 117 = 19.8$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-reg-quantile · level media · notes
Quantile regression with no features: an intercept $b$ is fitted to the $n = 7$ observations $y = (126; 143; 149; 88; 145; 130; 151)$ by minimizing $z(b) = \sum_i [\tau (y_i - b)^+ + (1 - \tau)(b - y_i)^+]$, with $\tau = 0.25$. What are the optimal intercept $\tilde b$ and the optimal value $\tilde z$?
  1. $\tilde b = 143$, $\tilde z = 67.75$
  2. $\tilde b = 130$, $\tilde z = 51.5$
  3. $\tilde b = 126$, $\tilde z = 50.5$
  4. $\tilde b = 88$, $\tilde z = 79$
Explanation. Without features the LP returns the empirical $\tau$-quantile: raising $b$ pays while the share of observations below it is at most $\tau$. Since $\tau n = 1.75$, $\tilde b$ is the observation in position 2 in increasing order, $126$, with $\tilde z = 50.5$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-reg-quantile · level media · notes
Quantile regression with no features: an intercept $b$ is fitted to the $n = 6$ observations $y = (148; 105; 87; 112; 149; 144)$ by minimizing $z(b) = \sum_i [\tau (y_i - b)^+ + (1 - \tau)(b - y_i)^+]$, with $\tau = 0.75$. What are the optimal intercept $\tilde b$ and the optimal value $\tilde z$?
  1. $\tilde b = 148$, $\tilde z = 36.75$
  2. $\tilde b = 112$, $\tilde z = 86.75$
  3. $\tilde b = 149$, $\tilde z = 37.25$
  4. $\tilde b = 144$, $\tilde z = 38.75$
Explanation. Without features the LP returns the empirical $\tau$-quantile: raising $b$ pays while the share of observations below it is at most $\tau$. Since $\tau n = 4.5$, $\tilde b$ is the observation in position 5 in increasing order, $148$, with $\tilde z = 36.75$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-reg-duale-interpolato · level media · notes
Quantile regression LP with no features: $\min\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$ subject to $b + u_i - v_i = y_i$, $u_i, v_i \ge 0$, $b$ free; $\tilde\pi_i$ is the optimal dual of the constraint of observation $i$ (it equals $\tau$ for observations above $\tilde b$, $-(1 - \tau)$ for those below, and $\sum_i\tilde\pi_i = 0$). Data: $y = (94; 101; 84; 87; 106)$, $\tau = 7/10$. What are $\tilde b$ and the dual $\tilde\pi$ of the interpolated observation ($y_i = \tilde b$)?
  1. $\tilde b = 101$, $\tilde\pi = -0.2$
  2. $\tilde b = 94$, $\tilde\pi = 0.7$
  3. $\tilde b = 101$, $\tilde\pi = 0.2$
  4. $\tilde b = 106$, $\tilde\pi = 0.2$
Explanation. $\tilde b$ is the empirical $\tau$-quantile: with $(k - 1)/n < \tau < k/n$ for $k = 4$ it is the 4th smallest value, $101$, with $3$ observations below and $1$ above. From $\sum_i\tilde\pi_i = 0$: $\tilde\pi = (1 - \tau)\cdot 3 - \tau\cdot 1 = 0.2$, inside $[-(1 - \tau), \tau]$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-reg-duale-interpolato · level media · notes
Quantile regression LP with no features: $\min\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$ subject to $b + u_i - v_i = y_i$, $u_i, v_i \ge 0$, $b$ free; $\tilde\pi_i$ is the optimal dual of the constraint of observation $i$ (it equals $\tau$ for observations above $\tilde b$, $-(1 - \tau)$ for those below, and $\sum_i\tilde\pi_i = 0$). Data: $y = (105; 87; 112; 149; 144; 133; 154)$, $\tau = 11/14$. What are $\tilde b$ and the dual $\tilde\pi$ of the interpolated observation ($y_i = \tilde b$)?
  1. $\tilde b = 149$, $\tilde\pi = 0.286$
  2. $\tilde b = 154$, $\tilde\pi = 0.286$
  3. $\tilde b = 149$, $\tilde\pi = -0.286$
  4. $\tilde b = 144$, $\tilde\pi = 0.786$
Explanation. $\tilde b$ is the empirical $\tau$-quantile: with $(k - 1)/n < \tau < k/n$ for $k = 6$ it is the 6th smallest value, $149$, with $5$ observations below and $1$ above. From $\sum_i\tilde\pi_i = 0$: $\tilde\pi = (1 - \tau)\cdot 5 - \tau\cdot 1 = 0.286$, inside $[-(1 - \tau), \tau]$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-reg-lad-3punti · level complessa · notes
Quantile regression LP with one feature ($p = 1$): $\min\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$ subject to $wx_i + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$, $u_i, v_i \ge 0$ ($u_i$: observation above the line, $v_i$: below), $w, b$ free, with $\tau = 0.5$ and three observations $(x_i; y_i)$: (1; 34), (4; 47), (5; 12). An optimal line interpolates $p + 1 = 2$ observations. What are the optimal slope $\tilde w$, intercept $\tilde b$ and optimal value?
  1. slope $4.33$, intercept $29.67$, value $19.67$
  2. slope $-5.5$, intercept $39.5$, value $29.5$
  3. slope $-35$, intercept $187$, value $59$
  4. slope $-5.5$, intercept $39.5$, value $14.75$
Explanation. Three candidate lines, one per pair of points; each leaves one residual $r$ on the third point, which costs $\tau r$ if the point is above ($r > 0$) and $(1 - \tau)|r|$ if below. Values: $19.67$, $14.75$, $59$. The cheapest is the line with slope $-5.5$ and intercept $39.5$.
Example 2 · lpm-g-reg-lad-3punti · level complessa · notes
Quantile regression LP with one feature ($p = 1$): $\min\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$ subject to $wx_i + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$, $u_i, v_i \ge 0$ ($u_i$: observation above the line, $v_i$: below), $w, b$ free, with $\tau = 0.5$ and three observations $(x_i; y_i)$: (1; 56), (3; 46), (8; 38). An optimal line interpolates $p + 1 = 2$ observations. What are the optimal slope $\tilde w$, intercept $\tilde b$ and optimal value?
  1. slope $-5$, intercept $61$, value $8.5$
  2. slope $-2.57$, intercept $58.57$, value $2.43$
  3. slope $-2.57$, intercept $58.57$, value $4.86$
  4. slope $-1.6$, intercept $50.8$, value $3.4$
Explanation. Three candidate lines, one per pair of points; each leaves one residual $r$ on the third point, which costs $\tau r$ if the point is above ($r > 0$) and $(1 - \tau)|r|$ if below. Values: $8.5$, $2.43$, $3.4$. The cheapest is the line with slope $-2.57$ and intercept $58.57$.
Example 1 · lpm-g-reg-obiettivo-retta · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP with one feature: $\min\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$ with $wx_i + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$, $u_i, v_i \ge 0$ ($u_i$: observation above the line, $v_i$: below). For the candidate line $w = 1$, $b = 110$ (not necessarily optimal), $\tau = 0.9$ and the observations $(x_i; y_i)$ (4; 117), (5; 125), (14; 112), (18; 138), what are $\sum_iu_i$, $\sum_iv_i$ and the objective value?
  1. $\sum u_i = 23$, $\sum v_i = 12$, value $13.1$
  2. $\sum u_i = 12$, $\sum v_i = 23$, value $21.9$
  3. $\sum u_i = 23$, $\sum v_i = 12$, value $35$
  4. $\sum u_i = 23$, $\sum v_i = 12$, value $21.9$
Explanation. Residuals $y_i - (wx_i + b)$: $3$, $10$, $-12$, $10$; positive parts sum to $23$ (the $u_i$), negative parts to $12$ (the $v_i$). Value $0.9\cdot 23 + 0.1\cdot 12 = 21.9$; swapping the weights of the two sides is the typical mistake.
Example 2 · lpm-g-reg-obiettivo-retta · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP with one feature: $\min\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$ with $wx_i + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$, $u_i, v_i \ge 0$ ($u_i$: observation above the line, $v_i$: below). For the candidate line $w = 4$, $b = 130$ (not necessarily optimal), $\tau = 0.25$ and the observations $(x_i; y_i)$ (4; 139), (9; 170), (13; 183), (19; 195), what are $\sum_iu_i$, $\sum_iv_i$ and the objective value?
  1. $\sum u_i = 5$, $\sum v_i = 18$, value $8.25$
  2. $\sum u_i = 18$, $\sum v_i = 5$, value $14.75$
  3. $\sum u_i = 5$, $\sum v_i = 18$, value $23$
  4. $\sum u_i = 5$, $\sum v_i = 18$, value $14.75$
Explanation. Residuals $y_i - (wx_i + b)$: $-7$, $4$, $1$, $-11$; positive parts sum to $5$ (the $u_i$), negative parts to $18$ (the $v_i$). Value $0.25\cdot 5 + 0.75\cdot 18 = 14.75$; swapping the weights of the two sides is the typical mistake.
Example 1 · lpm-g-reg-scorta · level facile · notes
A bakery forecasts daily demand from temperature $t$ (°C), price $p$ (€) and weekend indicator $e \in \{0, 1\}$ with quantile regression. Median model ($\tau = 0.5$): $274 - 3.5\,t - 26\,p + 32\,e$; model at the critical fractile $\tau = 9/13$ (the newsvendor quantile): $285 - 3.7\,t - 26\,p + 36\,e$. For a day with $t = 23$, $p = 3$, $e = 1$, how much should be produced, and what is the safety stock?
  1. produce $147.5$, safety stock $10.4$
  2. produce $157.9$, safety stock $157.9$
  3. produce $168.3$, safety stock $10.4$
  4. produce $157.9$, safety stock $10.4$
Explanation. One produces the forecast at the critical fractile, $157.9$; the median forecast is $147.5$, and their difference, $10.4$, is the safety stock, obtained with no assumption on the distribution of demand.
Example 2 · lpm-g-reg-scorta · level facile · notes
A bakery forecasts daily demand from temperature $t$ (°C), price $p$ (€) and weekend indicator $e \in \{0, 1\}$ with quantile regression. Median model ($\tau = 0.5$): $266 - 3.9\,t - 25\,p + 30\,e$; model at the critical fractile $\tau = 9/13$ (the newsvendor quantile): $276 - 4.1\,t - 27\,p + 30\,e$. For a day with $t = 13$, $p = 2.5$, $e = 0$, how much should be produced, and what is the safety stock?
  1. produce $152.8$, safety stock $2.4$
  2. produce $155.2$, safety stock $155.2$
  3. produce $155.2$, safety stock $2.4$
  4. produce $157.6$, safety stock $2.4$
Explanation. One produces the forecast at the critical fractile, $155.2$; the median forecast is $152.8$, and their difference, $2.4$, is the safety stock, obtained with no assumption on the distribution of demand.
